Nothing is lost (every wire is re-created from the code, the module re-emits byte-identically, and the shape is a fixed point from then on), and the alternative — giving the first part to the default — would collapse the far more common "unwired default + one mesh material" instead. `outputPartsFixes.test.ts` pins both halves, and the round-trip suite pins the fixed point. A lone unwired Output still emits `return vec3(1, 0, 0);` exactly as it always has. **An emitted part with NO channels is dropped by `buildShaderModule`** (`props.length > 0`), so an Output you have pointed at a mesh but not yet wired changes nothing in the preview — pre-existing, and deliberate: the alternative is that adding one throws every other mesh back onto its authored material to announce it. **It is MARKED rather than prevented** (`addedMaterialContributes` in utils/outputMaterials.ts — the `outputDefaultContributes` test applied to one material, over its own values and its own exposed list; `silent` in OutputNode, now scoped to a node that is neither the default nor PARKED, since after the split its old `index === 0 → false` form was simply unreachable): while such a node sets nothing, its mesh picker takes the orange `mesh-picker--silent` with its own sentence — this material sets no channel yet, so the meshes it names keep their current look — and every unset COLOUR row on it draws the UNSET swatch instead of the channel default — a white the preview never paints, which is what made "point an Output at a mesh" read as a colour assignment that silently failed (owner, 2026-09-18). Both clear themselves the moment anything on the node emits, and the channel default is truthful again then: a part that DOES emit really does render three's white for an unset `color`. The DEFAULT is never in this state — an Output contributing nothing emits the red sentinel, which `emitsNothing` marks instead, and that predicate is now scoped to the default node AND asked across every CONTRIBUTING node (both halves matter after the split). KNOWN and pre-existing: a driving Raymarch Output silences every plain Output, but `emitsNothing` does not consult it, so a silenced default can still show the red swatch. `outputSilentSection.test.ts` pins the predicate, both surfaces and the dropped part it describes. A part's material settings do not keep it alive either — the loader (0.6 and 0.8) skips a part with no channel (`hasChannels`), and emission mirrors that. (3) **Emission and the parse must land in ONE commit.** Emission alone is byte-stable, node-count-stable and `errors: []` while silently deleting every secondary binding on the first code-panel Apply — no pre-existing round-trip invariant can fail on it, so `outputParts.test.ts` asserts parts SURVIVE an Apply and that the graph does not grow across TWO successive ones. A targeted material with nothing wired still emits its `{}` entry, or the parse cannot re-create it and the material plus its edges vanish. **The pairing key is the BINDING, and that is the whole correctness of the resync since one Output node became one material** (`matchKey` in utils/resyncPairing.ts, extracted PURE so the failure is an executable attack rather than a source pin — `useSyncEngine` is a React hook and the vitest env is `node`). Every parsed Output is labelled the literal string `"Output"`, so the historical `registryType + label` key puts every one of them in ONE bucket and they pair by ARRAY ORDER — which on a multi-mesh document silently moves one material's id, position, exposedPorts, stored values and settings ONTO ANOTHER MESH, with `errors: []` and byte-identical emitted output. Nothing downstream notices: the module is the same text, the preview renders the same picture, and the only evidence is that the node the user was editing is now shading something else. The label carries no information for this type at all, so the binding REPLACES it rather than joining it (`output` plus `default` / `i<index>` / the SORTED names, so a material naming the same set in a different order is the same material — the parse rebuilds `meshTargets` in `parts` key order, which the user can change by re-ordering the code panel's text without meaning to re-bind anything). A Raymarch Output keeps the label form: it has no binding and the parse mints at most one. Two carries move with it. `carryInactiveSinks` carries a TARGETED plain Output ONLY when the parse produced no plain Output at all (`parseHasPlainOutput`, read off the PARSE and never off the old graph). Normally it does not — a targeted Output CONTRIBUTES, so the parse rebuilds it from the code, and carrying it too would add a node BESIDE the parse's copy, growing a GLB document by one Output per mesh ON EVERY APPLY while emitting each part twice. **That premise fails in exactly one state, and it cost the feature silently**: under a DRIVING Raymarch Output `graphToCode` empties the whole Output pass (`outputs = marchNode ? [] : contributingOutputs(nodes)`), so the module carries no `parts`, no `materialParts` and no top-level channels, nothing pairs, and every targeted Output plus its incoming edges was DELETED on Apply with `errors: []` — PARTIALLY, since the untargeted default came back through this very carry, so the graph went on working with some materials simply gone. The gate is what the PARSE holds rather than "does a march drive" for two reasons: a module with no plain Output has nothing a carried node could collide with BY CONSTRUCTION, which is what keeps the widening from re-opening the duplication above, and asking the old graph would resurrect a material the user had just deleted from the code panel by hand, on every Apply. It is NOT a complete answer to the march case, and the limit is the CALLER's: the carry runs inside `if (unpositioned.length === 0)`, so an Apply that also adds a node reaches none of it — one new `const` typed into the panel does that, and so does a feeder SHARED between the march and an Output, which `marchPartition` emits into both the per-step Fn and the flat body so the parse returns it twice and one copy is unpaired. And an unpaired plain Output is PLACED rather than handed to the relayout (`placeParsedOutputs`: below the lowest positioned Output, `UNFOLD_DY` apart, in emit order, so a built column and a restored column are the same column) — **at ROOT level and in ABSOLUTE coordinates, never adopting the anchor's frame**. It takes `oldNodes` as a third argument because that is the only place a positioned Output's frame still is: `mergeMatch` spreads the PARSED node and copies `id` and `position` alone, so `positioned` carries PARENT-RELATIVE coordinates with no `parentId` on them, and reading those as absolute put a new material a whole group-origin away while ranking them against a root Output's y picked the wrong anchor outright (`absoluteNodePosition`, utils/groupFrame.ts, resolves both). It does not INHERIT the frame for four reasons: `unpositioned.length > 0` sends it through `autoLayout`, which writes absolute positions while the group block that would re-add the frame is gated OFF on the same condition; that block restores `parentId` only for ids the OLD graph had, which a parsed id never is; a COLLAPSED anchor group hides members by className, so an adopted node would draw on top of the pill; and React Flow's own drop-into-group does the adoption, with the frame maths that goes with it, the moment the user drags it in — because the orphan-property carry, the inactive-sink carry and the ENTIRE group-preservation block are gated on `unpositioned.length === 0`, so ONE unpaired node DELETES EVERY GROUP FRAME on the canvas. That was tolerable while a new node meant one Multiply typed into the code panel; with one Output per material an ordinary GLB import mints a dozen at once. (4) **A part's discard is a KEY, never a statement** — a `Discard()` statement belongs to the module, so it is the DEFAULT material's cutout and lands on the DEFAULT node's bare `discard` handle — and `buildShaderModule` gives each part its own INDEXED `__partPixel<n>` wrapper (indexed because a mesh name is not an identifier: `Body.001` and `my mesh` are both legal). That builder also maps every part's inner keys through the SAME `CHANNEL_TO_PROP` table as the default; re-emitting them verbatim hands the loader keys it does not know and silently renders the default material on every mesh — right-looking source, wrong picture, no error. A part's wrapper falls back to the part's own **emissive** before white, exactly as the default path's `discardColor` does and for the same reason (the loader copies emissiveNode→colorNode only when colorNode is undefined, and a wrapper always defines it): falling straight to white made the ordinary glow-cutout wiring — Emissive + Discard, no Colour — render correctly on the default and wash out to lit white on a targeted material. **A material's Transparent / Side / Alpha clip / Depth write / Shading ride INSIDE its `parts` entry** (`engine/materialSettingsCode.ts`). The loader's `buildMaterial` has always applied all four per part (the frozen 0.6's lines 351-362, carried verbatim into 0.8's core), so this needed no loader change. What was missing was every step on the app side: graphToCode never wrote them, buildShaderModule's part loop dropped any key CHANNEL_TO_PROP did not know, and the parse wrote none. So an added material's settings did nothing (preview and export alike), and every Apply deleted them, because the mergeMatch carry only ever covered material 0. graphToCode now appends `materialSettingProps(material.materialSettings)` after a part's channels and discard, in the LOADER's spelling (`side: 2`), so the code panel, the module and a bare-script import speak one vocabulary. buildShaderModule re-validates the text with `materialSettingsFromSource` (the same sanitizer scriptToTSL uses) and re-emits it canonically after the channel props. That sanitizer strips a value's comments first (`settingValueText`), and the part loop reads a quoted key as the name it spells, because the parse does both: `transparent: true /* glass */` and `"transparent": true` otherwise reached the node but not the module, so after an Apply the preview rendered opaque under a node showing Transparent ticked. codeToGraph reads them back onto that material. There is ONE emitter (it also writes the default's top-level keys) and ONE text sanitizer, so the per-part rules cannot drift from the default's: alphaTest capped at 0.99, depthWrite:false only under transparency, a tampered side emits `side: 0`. Only those PART keys: `mergeVertices` is a module-level geometry directive (0.6.js:715; 0.8's `shouldWeld`), and `displacementMode` has no loader key, so a targeted node's Displacement rows follow the default — which is why both now render only on `moduleSettingsOutput`'s node rather than inertly on every one. **`flatShading` joined the part keys on 2026-09-18** (Blender's Shade Smooth / Shade Flat, a `Shading` select beside Side): it is a THREE.Material property three's node materials honour — `NodeBuilder.isFlatShading()` swaps `normalViewGeometry` for `normalFlat`, a face normal from the DERIVATIVES of the view position, so it needs no vertex normals and works on a welded displaced primitive — and it is per-material, so two materials on one model may differ. Only the FLAT case is stored and emitted (smooth is three's default), which keeps every graph and export made before it byte-identical. **0.8 applies it and the frozen 0.6 does NOT**: 0.6 reads its known keys by name and ignores the rest, so such a shader renders SMOOTH on an old loader rather than failing — `perMeshMaterials.test.ts` pins that split in both directions, and `shaderloader08Core.test.ts` drives 0.8 against a real three material. Settings that emit nothing leave the part byte-identical. The DEFAULT's settings still never appear in editor code (they ride buildShaderModule's options), so **the resync carries them PER KEY** (`carryMaterialSettings` in utils/resyncPairing.ts) rather than as the whole-object overwrite it was: the four `PART_SETTING_KEYS` are CODE-AUTHORITATIVE on a TARGETED node, since graphToCode writes them inside that node's `parts` entry — unticking Transparent in the code panel and pressing Apply must not have the old node put it back — while an UNTARGETED one inherits them, because the parse can never have seen them; and `displacementMode` / `mergeVertices` are ALWAYS carried, since neither is ever in the editor code for any node, which closes a known loss for a targeted material. It is always a FRESH object and never a mutation of either side: ShaderPreview, CodeEditor and the hook all subscribe to `materialSettings` BY REFERENCE and bail on `Object.is`, so an in-place update leaves the preview and the A-Frame tab showing the old settings with no error. Both sides are read as ADVERSARIAL (the old node's settings come out of a `.fastshader` or the autosave), and absent on both sides stays absent, so a node that never had settings gains no key. Two parse rules are load-bearing. First, the four keys are never channels: `side: color2.x` would otherwise mint a Split node and a dead `side` edge on that node, and `side: color2` would wire one straight in (an unresolvable value such as `THREE.DoubleSide` wires nothing either way, so it cannot test the rule). Second, a part whose body carries only settings counts as EMPTY for the byte-identical merge, or two freshly added materials both set Transparent would collapse into one. `outputPartSettings.test.ts` pins all of it. (5) **Mesh names are a REJECT-list, not an ASCII whitelist** (`isUsableMeshName`): three's `PropertyBinding.sanitizeNodeName` strips only `[ ] . : /` and maps whitespace to `_`, so non-ASCII SURVIVES — measured, `Ķermenis_āda 2` → `Ķermenis_āda_2`. A `[A-Za-z0-9_.-]` whitelist would make every Latvian/CJK mesh permanently untargetable in a Latvian-first app while admitting `Body.001`, which the glTF loader can never produce. Refused instead: empty, control chars/U+2028-9, over-length, and `__proto__` — and every map keyed by a mesh name is a `Map` or null-prototype object, because a plain-object lookup resolves `toString`/`constructor` to FUNCTIONS and assigns one as `node.material` (measured in a browser). Names are validated on every restore path (`loadGraph`, `applyProjectToStore`, `loadSavedGroups` — a saved group may contain the Output — plus a re-run over the COMBINED list in `instantiateSavedGroup`, since a group's claim is only checkable against the graph it lands in) AND again at emission, the gate that decides what becomes code. **`parts` is keyed by MESH, so a material naming N meshes emits N entries carrying the same expressions** — the channels are resolved ONCE per material, not once per mesh. `codeToGraph` merges byte-identical part bodies back into one material naming several meshes, which is what makes the feature survive its own round trip (without it a three-mesh material split into three NODES on the first Apply, each with a copy of the wiring). EMPTY bodies are deliberately NOT merged: they carry nothing to match on, and two freshly added mesh materials — the state right before you wire them differently — are exactly the pair that would be collapsed. The flip side is inherent: two SEPARATE materials that happen to be wired identically merge into one, which renders the same and shows one node instead of two. **A mesh belongs to exactly ONE material: ticking it MOVES it, now across NODES** (`assignMeshTargetsAcross`, the single writer behind the picker). No row is ever disabled for being claimed by another node — locking them was what made swapping two materials' meshes impossible short of deleting one and rebuilding its wiring — and moving makes the swap the ordinary two clicks. Because "another material" is another NODE, this is necessarily a MULTI-NODE write: `updateNodeData` patches one node, so doing the move as two writes would let Cmd+Z step through a half-assigned state where two Outputs both claim the mesh. It is ONE `setNodes` inside ONE `asOneHistoryEntry`, with the no-op guard OUTSIDE that bracket (`beginInteraction` snapshots AND clears `future` up front, so bracketing a write that changes nothing costs an undo entry and destroys the redo stack). It is pure and returns the SAME array when nothing changed, the reference contract every graph-shaped helper here holds. An INDEX-bound node is never re-targeted and never stripped: it is bound to a glTF MATERIAL and has no names to take, and a mesh ticked elsewhere that its material also covers is an OVERRIDE (the name claim wins, loader 0.8's precedence), not a move. The ONE refused row is an UNTICKED mesh past the per-node cap (`MAX_PARTS` = 9 names; `materialTargetNames` caps on read, so a tenth tick used to vanish silently): `.mesh-picker__row--capped`, `aria-disabled`, never `disabled` (WebKit drops the title), with the reason on hover. Unticking is never refused. So the UI can no longer author a duplicate at all; one still ARRIVES from a hand-edited or foreign file, where emission resolves it first-claim-wins per MESH across the whole node set and the shadowed node is MARKED. **Unticking the LAST mesh makes the node UNTARGETED, which is a different state from the old "empty section"** — it is a whole-model material again, and therefore either the default or PARKED (see the several-outputs convention). `assignMeshTargetsAcross` DELETES the `meshTargets` key rather than storing `[]`, so such a node is JSON-identical to one that was never targeted. That is the only way back from a targeted node to a whole-model one, which is why EVERY plain Output's picker offers "All meshes (default)": it was material-0-only while several materials shared a node, where a second empty list would have authored a second default that silently loses at emission, and among NODES the active flag resolves that ambiguity instead. **The caps are now two different things.** `MAX_PARTS` (9) is the picker's per-NODE mesh-name cap — a per-NODE constant in all three of its meanings (names per material, named sections per node, picker rows) and NEVER a module budget. **The PARSE counts ENTRIES against `MAX_PART_ENTRIES` too**, exactly as `buildIndexOutputs` already counted against `MAX_INDEX_MATERIALS`: it read `MAX_PARTS` until 2026-09-18, i.e. 9 against emission's 90, so an ordinary twelve-material GLB lost three materials and their wiring on the FIRST code-panel Apply — `severity: 'warning'` does not block, `carryInactiveSinks` deliberately does not carry a TARGETED Output, and the next graph→code pass then wrote the truncation back over the user's source. The body-merge branch starts a SIBLING at the per-node name cap rather than over-filling one node, which is the shape emission produces for two identically wired materials, so the document is a fixed point from the first Apply instead of storing names `materialTargetNames` trims on read. Emission's cap is `MAX_PART_ENTRIES` (90), and it is a RUNNING TOTAL across every contributing node inside ONE `planNamedPartsAcross` call, because `parts` is a single map in a single module: a mesh claimed by an earlier-ranked node must not be re-claimed by a later one, and the cap bounds that map rather than each node's share of it. `MAX_INDEX_MATERIALS` (16) is the index twin, counted APART from the named cap everywhere (`countSectionsAcross`). The SANITIZER still caps PER NODE and never across the list, and that is what keeps `instantiateSavedGroup` safe: it sanitizes the COMBINED live-graph + arriving group list, so a cross-node cap there could delete a LIVE node — an over-cap entry is instead left out of the MODULE, never out of the graph. `planNamedPartsAcross` is the ONE first-claim-wins loop, shared by emission and the node's shadowed mark, and it runs the same `claimNamedParts` body as the per-node form, so the golden that pins that body against graphToCode's pre-split loop still covers what emission executes. Every restore path COUNTS what it drops (`sanitizeOutputMaterialsReport`: entries of a legacy `materials` array past the cap or not objects, names unusable or past a node's cap, an index binding the signature cannot vouch for — de-duplication is not a loss) and raises `output-sections-trimmed`, a toggle-less LimitModal notice whose WORDS say Output NODES: the stored slots through App's mount effect (which also writes a repaired library back, so it fires once), an opened file directly from `applyProjectToStore`. **The emitted key is `JSON.stringify` plus TWO escapes, and neither is optional**: the star-slash, because the exported `.js` carries the project block as a block comment; and `<` → `\u003C`, because the generated module is inlined into an HTML `<script>` by `tslToPreviewHTML`, and the tokenizer ends that element at the first `</script` in the raw text no matter that it sits inside a JS string literal. A mesh name is attacker-chosen and `isUsableMeshName` admits `<` and `/` by design, so the one-escape version let a name spelling `x</script><svg onload=…>` close the script and run as live markup — contained in the sandboxed preview by its opaque origin, but the XR popup is a TOP-LEVEL document at the app's REAL origin (verified in Chromium: `executed=true` before, no injected element after). `tslToPreviewHTML` escapes the whole embedded module the same way — a deliberate second copy, because the sink covers every OTHER string reaching that document while the source-side escape covers the key wherever else it is written; `outputPartsFixes.test.ts` pins each half independently. Both escapes preserve the string's VALUE, so the loader's exact-name dispatch is untouched. The parts SPLITTER may not use `indexOf(':')` — a mesh name can contain a colon (OBJ names skip three's sanitizer, so `g Char:Body` really lands as `Char:Body`), and splitting at the first one cut inside the JSON key and dropped the part silently, with the mesh falling back to the default material and NO ⚠, because the name IS in the inventory. It also accepts an UNQUOTED key, since the code panel is a real editing surface and `parts: { Glass: {…} }` is valid JS the parse already takes. (6) **The inventory is session-only and NEVER persisted.** `previewMeshInventory` holds what the SANDBOX reported (`fs:model-meshes`, keyed `custom:<id>` so a report from a torn-down document is identifiably stale) — the trusted side may not parse model bytes, and sub-mesh names are not knowable from the file anyway since GLTFLoader rewrites and de-dupes them. It is forgeable (the preview runs the loaded shader) so it is sanitized on arrival, and a persisted copy would let a tampered `fs:graph` vouch for meshes no loaded model contains. An over-long name is reported EMPTY rather than truncated: a truncated one would be offered, targeted, emitted, and then match nothing at runtime while the node showed no ⚠ (the truncated name IS in the inventory it checks itself against). **The mesh row appears as soon as a MODEL is on screen**, not once the sandbox has reported: `showMeshRow`'s `hasMeshes` reads `previewMeshInventory`, which `commitGlbImport → setPreviewMesh` has just NULLED and which can only come back from the rebuilt preview iframe after the 200 ms debounce, an A-Frame boot and a multi-mesh glTF parse — so the DEFAULT Output rendered with no mesh control at all for seconds after every GLB import, and permanently when no mesh has a usable name or the Model menu is switched to a primitive. `!!shownMesh` gates VISIBILITY only; the option LIST is still the inventory's alone, because `meshInventory` is the one source of mesh TARGETS and the glTF reader's PREDICTED names are display-only by construction. **The node is the authoring surface, and the NODE is the scope**: every plain Output carries ONE mesh picker (`nodes/MeshTargetPicker.tsx`) as the first row under its header — the header says only the node's name, because which mesh it shades is what that row says. It is a button plus a PORTALLED checkbox list, not a `<select>`: one material may shade several meshes, and a native `<select multiple>` has no checkboxes, a fixed-height list box, and modifier-click semantics nobody discovers. Portalled for the reason every other menu here is — a list inside the node would scale with the canvas zoom, paint under the nodes below it, and grow the node's own box while open, moving every socket beneath it mid-gesture. The CLOSED control shows the FIRST mesh plus an ellipsis (`.mesh-picker__more`, a SEPARATE span so the label's own `text-overflow` clip cannot eat the "there are more" signal exactly when a long name makes it matter), and it says "All meshes" where the list row says "All meshes (default)" — at the 104px cap the longer string clipped to "All meshes (…", which reads as a cut-off NAME rather than as the default state, while the 260px list can afford to say which one it is. It reads **"Not rendered"** on a PARKED node and **"Nothing left"** on a default that shades no mesh of the model on screen, each dimming the node's channel rows with it: the picker IS where those two marks and their sentences live, which is why `showMeshRow` keeps the row up for a parked node whatever the model situation. That condition is otherwise "a model is loaded, or this node names a mesh, or it is index-bound" — so the row stays visible on a graph reopened without its model, where a target that still emits must not be stranded. An INDEX-bound node shows a read-only `IndexSectionChip` INSTEAD of a picker: it is bound to a glTF MATERIAL, so it has no mesh list to edit — the model decides which meshes wear that material, and restyling one of them means adding a mesh-targeted Output, which overrides it. The mesh NAME is bold and 11.7px (9 × 1.3) on the node and in the list alike, so it outranks the channel labels around it; the control's 20px height is sized for it (at 16px the descenders clipped), and it is width-capped for the reason `outputTargetChip.test.ts` documents: the node is `width: fit-content` inside React Flow's abspos wrapper, which resolves to MAX-CONTENT, so `min-width: 0` alone does not stop an attacker-supplied name stretching the node past a thousand pixels. **Four controls of the stacked shape are GONE, each with a replacement.** "+ Add output" (owner decision D3: the palette tile and Shift+A already add an Output and its own picker targets it — which also deleted two cap sentences that had stopped describing anything, invented no position logic, and fired no new `evalLog('node-add')` into a study-pinned chokepoint). The per-material ✕ (delete the node). `.output-node__divider` (the node's own `--cat-output` border does that job; `.output-node__subdivider` survives as the Pixel/Vertex line). And `data-material-index` (see the right-click rule below). `ShaderSettingsMenu` is titled **Material Settings** (2026-09-18) and takes a `nodeId` and nothing else — the title is factual for 13 of its 19 rows, and the other five say so: Total Cost, Budget and the texture-memory line sit under a **"Shader (whole document)"** heading (the cost walk seeds every contributing Output and the budget is a device setting), while the two Displacement rows render ONLY on `moduleSettingsOutput`'s node — `mergeVertices` is a module-level geometry directive and `displacementMode` has no loader key, so anywhere else those checkboxes wrote a field nothing read, which is a gap this closes rather than creates. The section heading over Transparent/Side/Alpha clip/Depth write is **Rendering**, not a third "Material" stacked under the title and the scope line. The English literal IS the i18n key, so lv.json moved with it — the node the user right-clicked IS the material, so its channels, its stored values and its material settings are that node's own fields, and the section selector with its `contextMenu.materialIndex` seed went with the stack it navigated. It keeps a STATIC scope line (the `Material` label plus `formatSectionLabel`, never a dropdown), rendered only while SEVERAL Output nodes exist, when "which one am I editing" is a real question; the MESH stays deliberately uneditable there, because the node's own picker owns that binding and a second control for one binding is how the two end up disagreeing. **A node whose EVERY named mesh is absent from the loaded model is DORMANT** (`dormantIndicesForPreview` in outputMaterials.ts, asked about the node's own material and read as `nodeDormant`): the node collapses to its header plus a one-line chip whose title names the meshes it waits for, and comes back — wiring intact — the moment a model carrying those names loads. Drop a different model and the multimesh setup clears itself; load the matching one and it restores, because this is a pure VISIBILITY rule over the session-only inventory: data, edges, history and EMISSION never change (emission may never depend on the inventory — it is absent after every reload and forgeable by the sandboxed preview), which is what makes the restore perfect by construction. The node is never HIDDEN, only collapsed: the wiring behind it must not read as having been deleted. The DEFAULT never sleeps, and after the split that is a consequence rather than a special case — it has no binding that could be absent, which is what "material 0 never hides" always really rested on. **Two context rules ride in the shared helper and may not be bypassed**: an UNREPORTED custom model (`previewMesh` set, inventory null — every swap passes through this window) hides NOTHING, or the chip claims "for another model" about the very model that is loading; and the loader's single-mesh fallback (0.6 and 0.8 alike) is MIRRORED — a parts-only module (the default contributes nothing, `outputDefaultContributes`, which shares `storedValueEmits` with the red-fallback swatch) on a ONE-mesh model paints the FIRST part, so that node is actively shading the screen and stays VISIBLE with its missing names marked (hiding what is being rendered would make the chip a lie). That exemption needs the `firstNamedHere` opt, resolved by `firstNamedOutputId` in EMIT order and never array order: the rule is about the MODULE's first part, so without it every targeted node exempts ITSELF and none ever sleeps. **A dormant node unmounts its whole handle block**, so dormancy folds into the `useUpdateNodeInternals` key (`nodeDormant ? '~' : exposedPorts.join('|')`; `~` cannot collide with a joined port list, whose ids are word characters) — without that the remounted handles of a waking node are never re-measured and every wire restored with it stays undrawn until a reload. Two more guards exist because a stored edge now legitimately targets an ABSENT handle, the first steady state of that kind in the codebase: `findNearestEdge` skips an edge whose NAMED handle fails to resolve in measured bounds (what is not DRAWN is not hit-testable — `handleAnchor`'s first-handle fallback otherwise measured a phantom curve that drop-on-edge could SPLICE with no highlight ever painted), and React Flow's 008 warning is swallowed only for a wire landing on a currently-dormant Output. **That suppression is keyed on the EDGE ID, not on the handle** (`components/NodeEditor/flowErrors.ts`, a zero-import leaf, plus `outputEdgeIsDormant`). The old rule regexed the `m<n>:` prefix out of the warning text, which a bare handle carries no trace of; React Flow's 008 message (verified verbatim in `@xyflow/system`) also carries the EDGE id, so the leaf extracts that and the predicate asks the node the wire actually LANDS ON — resolved by looking the edge up in the store's own `edges` array, which NodeEditor passes to React Flow BY REFERENCE, never by parsing the composite id (`generateEdgeId` joins four fields with `-` and two of them are handles out of a `.fastshader`, so it has no unambiguous reverse). It is strictly NARROWER than what it replaced — the old rule asked "is material n dormant on ANY Output", so with two Outputs each holding a material 1, one asleep, it excused a genuine 008 about the awake one — and it refuses a `source`-side message outright, since an absent SOURCE handle is always the missing-`useUpdateNodeInternals` bug this must never hide. Its tests format every message through `@xyflow/system`'s own `errorMessages` rather than a transcription, so a React Flow bump that renames a word fails loudly instead of silently disarming the suppression — which in production looks like a sleeping node flooding the console again. One consequence is ACCEPTED and documented rather than fixed: a dormant node's feeder chain renders as a wireless island (the wiring is intact, and deleting those nodes as junk is the risk the chip's title mitigates). **Import-built INDEX bindings** (GLB Phase 5 Step 5; `utils/outputMaterials.ts`, `engine/partKeyLiteral.ts`). NB the word "section" survives below, and on screen, as the name for ONE material's block of channels — which is now a whole node, but the dormant chip and the settings menu still word it that way ("material section(s) for another model"), so the two vocabularies meet here. An Output NODE may be bound to a glTF MATERIAL INDEX instead of mesh names: `gltfMaterialIndex` at NODE level, beside `modelSignature: { materials }` (the model's glTF material names in order, '' for an unnamed one, read by the trusted-side reader at import, validated by the ONE `sanitizeModelSignature`) and `modelMeshes: { name, material }[]` (the certain GLTFLoader mesh names of each material, `mirrorNamesByMaterial`, the loader-0.6 MIRROR source). **The signature is REPLICATED on every index node** — dormancy is decided per NODE, so a node without its own copy could never tell whether it applies to the loaded model — and the sanitizer DETACHES any index node whose copy differs. **`modelMeshes` is the opposite: ONE WHOLE LIST on the lowest-`emitRank` index node, never split per material.** `gltfSectionBuilder` fills it in FIRST-SCENE-APPEARANCE order, which INTERLEAVES materials, and `materialPartsMirrorPlan` walks that stored order straight into module TEXT — regrouping it by material would silently reorder the mirror keys of every already-distributed single-GLB export. Eleven rules. (1) Index and names are EXCLUSIVE: `materialTargetNames` returns [] for an index section, so `parts` emission, the name dormancy rule and the pickers all see it as nameless, and `assignMeshTargetsAcross` leaves it untouched by reference and refuses to target one. The DEFAULT is never an index node: it stays UNTARGETED and empty, and every built glTF material, index 0 included, is a node of its own — a targeted default would paint every unclaimed mesh on 0.6 or on a signature mismatch. (2) The signature exists only while an index section does, and `modelMeshes` only beside a signature, so a graph without index sections gains no key and emits byte-identically. (3) **Emission** goes through `planIndexPartsAcross`, the index twin of `planNamedPartsAcross` (first claim wins by glTF index; inside the signature only; ascending; `MAX_INDEX_MATERIALS` = 16 as a RUNNING TOTAL across the node set, counted APART from the named cap everywhere — `countSectionsAcross`). The ONE signature governing the table is `moduleSignatureOf`, the copy carried by the lowest-ranked CONTRIBUTING node holding an index binding — asked by graphToCode, by the Output node and by the single-GLB export plan alike (`glbExportPlan` re-spelled its body inline until 2026-09-18), so the node's red-sentinel test cannot disagree with what emission really writes: the editor TSL gains `materialParts: { "<i>": { … } }, modelSignature: { materials: [ … ] }` on the ONE return line, bodies built by the same `partBody` as `parts`, keys and names through `moduleStringLiteral`. An unwired index section still emits `"<i>": {  }`, or the parse could not re-create it. (4) **The parse lands in the SAME commit**: `buildIndexOutputs` mints one NODE per index FIRST, then `buildPartOutputs` the named ones — the canonical order (the default at rank 0, then `materialParts` ascending, then `parts`) — with NO body merge, keys only canonical decimal inside a valid signature (a numeric literal counts only when spelled exactly so; `1.0` is refused), the whole table refused, with a warning, without a valid signature, and a warning past the cap. Every node it makes carries the signature, replicated exactly as the unfold and the sanitizer replicate it. `materialParts`, `modelSignature` and `materialPartsMirror` are never wired as channels. Each node's `emitOrder` is its MINT position and is WRITTEN, never left absent, so a parsed document and a restored one describe their order identically and an Apply cannot renumber one into the other's shape — and it is that rank, never a node id or an array position, that namespaces each material's synthetic channel variables (`_part<rank>_color`), or two Applies of one module would pick different synthetic names and a deferred `Discard` naming one would resolve onto the wrong node. (5) **Mirrors are MODULE-only (R7)**: `buildShaderModule` writes a `parts` entry per `materialPartsMirrorPlanAcross` name whose index emitted a body — the plan reads `modelMeshes` off the lowest-ranked index node, with the cap and the name-claim set as UNIONS across every Output, since both bound ONE module — its body byte-identical to that index entry, never for a name a named section holds, capped at `MAX_MIRROR_ENTRIES`, each listed in `materialPartsMirror`, and `parts: {}` at minimum (R1). The plan comes from NODE data, never from the session's `previewMesh` (emission may not depend on what is loaded), and every module path passes the same one: `exportShader`, the code panel's tabs, the preview module and its XR popup (`tslToPreviewHTML`'s `materialPartsMirror` option when no `shaderModule` is handed in) — each through a cheap `mirrorPlanKey` selector. A dev build throws on any `checkLegacyGuard` violation, so the whole emission corpus is guarded in CI; production never runs it. (6) The module's signature is re-read as JSON text (`parseModelSignatureText`) and re-sanitized; a signature that is not JSON drops the table whole (R3). (7) **`modelMeshes` survive an Apply by carry, not by parse**: `useSyncEngine`'s mergeMatch calls `carryModelMeshes`, which copies them only while the PARSED signature equals the old one — a signature edited in the code panel describes a different model. `scriptToTSL` drops `materialPartsMirror` and the entries it names (R6). (8) **An index-bound node draws a read-only chip** (`IndexSectionChip`, a `<span>` reusing `.mesh-picker` plus `--material`, so the 104 px width cap holds for an attacker-supplied name) labelled with the display-sanitized material name (`displayMaterialName`: control and bidi characters become U+FFFD, capped at 64 code points) or, unnamed, "Material #<index>" (0-based, as in the glTF JSON); a duplicate index dims it with a title saying so. It is READ-ONLY and carries no ✕: the way to remove this material is to delete the node, which takes its signature and its mirror list with it. `emitsNothing` is false whenever any contributing node emits, so the red sentinel swatch never sits beside an object-return module. (9) The sanitizer (every restore path) keeps an index binding only inside a valid signature and strips names from it (counted), DETACHES an invalid one into an empty named node with its wiring (counted), keeps duplicates, and cleans `modelMeshes` (usable unique names, integer materials inside the signature, `MAX_MODEL_MESHES`). **It PRESERVES a valid NODE-LEVEL `gltfMaterialIndex`**, and that one word is a data-loss the split introduced: it used to delete that key unconditionally — correct while material 0 was never an index section — so the load AFTER the one that split an import-built document would have destroyed every index node and its signature SILENTLY, leaving a document of blank untargeted Outputs. The unfold writes the key on boot 1 and the autosave stores it, so the pin is that the split shape survives a SECOND load unchanged, arrays included. (10) **Dormancy is trusted-side and all-or-nothing per node** (GLB Phase 5 Step 6): `createPreviewMesh` stores the reader's facts as `PreviewMesh.gltf` (the signature plus predicted mesh name → material indices; computed for every glb/gltf load, eval included, where no index section can arise; never cached, so a restore recomputes them), `loadedModelOf(previewMesh)` reads them as `none`/`not-gltf`/`unknown`/`gltf`, and `indexSectionsAwake` is true only when the node's signature `modelSignatureMatches` the loaded one (raw names, never the display-sanitized ones), or when the loaded glTF's facts are `unknown` (a refused model hides nothing). With no custom model or an OBJ the sections sleep behind the dormant chip, which then says "material section(s) for another model" and names them. `dormantIndicesForPreview` takes `indexSectionsAwake` as a REQUIRED opt, applied independently of the inventory hold-off (the facts exist the moment the model is loaded), and the two whole-store derivations compute it (`indexAwakeFor`), so PreviewLink and the scoped 008 swallow follow with no edit of their own. Never from `fs:model-meshes` or the loader's `shader-material-parts` event, both forgeable. Visibility only: emission never reads `previewMesh`. The facts count only while the pane SHOWS the model: ShaderPreview writes the session-only store flag `previewShowsModel` (its rendered geometry is `'custom'`), and `shownPreviewMesh` reads `previewMesh` as absent when it is false, so picking a primitive (or a built-in, or a march window taking over) while a glTF stays loaded sleeps the index sections (name sections still hide nothing then, the inventory being cleared). (11) **The double claim** (`indexSectionCoverageAcross`, over `planNamedPartsAcross` and `planIndexPartsAcross`): each index node's meshes are the loaded model's predicted names wearing its material; one a NAME section claims is overridden (the name claim wins, 0.8's precedence), so the chip's title lists what it shades and what a mesh section took, dims when every mesh is overridden (or the index is a duplicate), and is marked `--missing` when no mesh wears the material. The name picker's row carries "Also in material section “X” — a mesh section wins" (`indexClaimed`), built across every contributing node's coverage. Nothing is refused. `pickFreeMesh` / `pickFreeMeshAcross` (a mesh no node covers first, else an index-covered one, which then becomes an override) have NO production caller since "+ Add output" retired — they are kept as tested primitives, like the other materials-form plans, for whatever seeds the next add gesture. The predicted names may be uncertain; that affects these titles only. **Every Output node carries its own decorative PREVIEW SOCKET** on its right border — one per node, a node being one material — and PreviewLink anchors that node's wire on it, so moving it in CSS moves the wire with no second rule to keep in step. It is never a React Flow Handle: the Output has no outputs and a real handle would invite a wire that can never land. Drawn at exactly twice `--handle-size` (so it follows the coarse-pointer bump too) in `--node-cost-text`, the WIRE's own colour rather than a literal black — that token is the auto-contrast value NodeEditor publishes from the user-picked canvas background, so socket and wire match on the default light canvas (both black) AND on a dark one, where a hardcoded black socket would vanish while the wire went white; it is read ONLY inside `.react-flow`, because NodeEditor publishes it on `.node-editor`, which the asset bar also sits inside, so an unscoped read turned the palette TILE's socket white-on-white. **Whether it is a CONTROL depends on the node's binding** (owner decision D2). On an UNTARGETED Output it is a `<button>` carrying `nodrag` with its pointerdown stopped, so a press activates without dragging, panning or selecting — solid while active, hollow while parked, and the hollow state is drawn with an INSET shadow rather than a border so the outer white rim and the disc's size stay identical and only the fill changes. On a TARGETED or index-bound one it is an INERT `<span>`: activation only means anything among UNTARGETED Outputs, so a click here would write a flag `normalizeActiveOutput` then strips and — worse — would silently stop the whole-model material emitting. It is drawn SOLID, because such a node always contributes and hollow only ever means parked, and it keeps REAL pointer-events so its `title` can open (TooltipLayer resolves its host from `elementFromPoint`, so a title on a `pointer-events: none` element never fires), with `cursor: default` the only signal that it is not a button. The BASE rule is `pointer-events: none` and only `.react-flow` opts back in, because the palette tiles and the node-editor overview render this same class as an inert `<span>` inside `pointer-events: none` cards, and a control must never be reachable from a static replica. **The right-click scope is the NODE**: `onNodeContextMenu` passes the node id alone, and the `data-material-index` walk that used to pick a SECTION out of a stack has nothing left to choose between. `openContextMenu`'s three pin fields became ONE named object (`pin?: ContextMenuPin`) rather than shifting its 8th positional argument — that slot sat immediately before `sourceHandleType`, and a slide there would silently flip the wire-drop direction, which renders as nothing while still emitting code. **Cost is seeded from the whole CONTRIBUTING SET** (`costSeeds` in utils/nodeCost.ts: a driving Raymarch Output alone, else `contributingOutputs`), unioned inside ONE `computeReachableCost` walk, so a feeder shared by two materials is counted once and the total never exceeds the sum of the per-node prices. Seeding one sink was right while one node held every material; with per-material nodes it would have priced one mesh's chain and left the rest free — and it also replaces `activeSink`'s retired last-resort term, since a document whose every Output is targeted has no sink at all and used to price at 0 with an inert cost pill while graphToCode emitted its parts perfectly. Each sink still carries its OWN badge (`sinkCosts` — what the shader would cost with THAT node driving, muted to 0.45 opacity on an inactive one so it reads as a comparison figure rather than as the meter's total), and an Output's badge is drawn at TWICE the usual size (owner request 2026-09-18: an Output's number is not one node's price among fifty, it is what the shader would cost with that sink driving). That rule is SCOPED `.react-flow`, the cost-badge-colour precedent, and here the scope is load-bearing rather than tidy: `NodePreviewCard` renders this exact markup for the palette tile, which lives outside the canvas inside `.content-browser__items`' `overflow-y: hidden`, and that never measures an absolutely-positioned badge, so an unscoped rule would silently clip the tile's number. `top` moves with the size (the badge is `line-height: 1`, so 12px/-14px leaves 2px of clearance and 24px keeps that 2px at -26px), and 26px was CHECKED against the group frame's badge band and FITS with 8-12px of slack — so `BADGE_CLEARANCE` does not have to move, which matters because raising it would re-lay-out every built-in group's committed snapshot. In POINTS PER PIXEL the summed total is an UPPER bound (a pixel runs exactly one material), while it UNDER-counts the other currency: compile work, pipelines and memory (every material compiles its own pipeline, shared nodes included, and texture memory is not priced at all). Two Image nodes holding the same image price twice (two samples), while the module builds them ONE texture when their texture settings (colour space, filter, repeat, flipY) match, and one image element either way; see the Image node bullet. NOT done yet: the preview-pane assignment TABLE, a per-material displacement mode (a part follows the default's), and real per-part budgeting in the CostBar (needs a ShaderCarousel calibration entry). A bare-script import (a `.js` with no project block) of a parts module still does not reverse the node-prop names inside `parts` (`colorNode:` lands on a dead `colorNode` handle on that node) — part settings pass through that path intact, the channels do not. **Never verified on a screen**: whether a 16-material GLB import reads as a usable column of cards or as a wall of them (`UNFOLD_DY` is 180 against a one-material Output's ~137px estimate, so such an import unfolds into a ~2900px column), and what N Output nodes each carrying a replicated `modelSignature` do to `fs:graph` in WebKit, which charges localStorage by the STORED WIDTH — measure a 16-material document with `scripts/verify-webkit-storage.mjs` before shipping.

- **Canvas navigation — the ONE model, by input device.** Everything below is an implementation detail of these four sentences; if a change makes one of them false on any platform, the change is wrong.
  - **Mouse (macOS and Windows alike): the WHEEL ZOOMS, the MIDDLE BUTTON PANS.** Wheel-to-zoom is React Flow's `zoomOnScroll`, which the custom wheel handler falls through to (see the next bullet for how a mouse wheel is told from a trackpad); middle-drag is `panOnDrag={DESKTOP_PAN_ON_DRAG}` = `[1, 2]`, i.e. middle AND right button — right is included because it costs nothing and some users reach for it, while LEFT stays free for marquee selection and node dragging.
  - **iPad and touchscreen: TWO FINGERS PAN, PINCH ZOOMS.** There are no wheel events on a real touch screen, so the gesture is re-created from raw pointers by the touch-nav effect: centroid drag = pan, finger distance = pinch-zoom anchored to the pinch centre. Unambiguous, because the raw pointers say how many fingers are down.
  - **Trackpad: the user SAYS so — `trackpadScroll`, right-click the toolbar → "Trackpad scrolling".** With it on, a two-finger vertical swipe pans (pinch still zooms, which is the trackpad's native zoom anyway); with it off, the mouse model above applies. Two-finger HORIZONTAL pans in both modes — `deltaX` on its own is unambiguous whatever the device — and pinch / `Ctrl`/`Cmd`+wheel pass straight through to React Flow in both, so **zoom is never unreachable**.
  - **Why a setting and not a device sniff.** A trackpad's two-finger swipe and a mouse's wheel notch arrive as the SAME event. A heuristic was built (`wheelKind.ts`: pixel-vs-line delta mode, then the legacy `wheelDelta` detent of 120), shipped, and REVERTED, because it read a real macOS mouse as a trackpad and took that user's zoom away — almost certainly because macOS accelerates mouse wheels too, so `wheelDelta` is rarely a clean multiple of 120, and the per-gesture latch let one such event poison a whole scroll. **Two properties make the class unsafe to guess at**: the failure is ASYMMETRIC (misreading a trackpad costs a pan you repeat; misreading a mouse removes a primary control), and it is UNTESTABLE from here — CDP's `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` hardcodes `wheelDeltaY` to ±120 whatever delta you request, and a constructed `WheelEvent` leaves it at 0, so neither reproduces the hardware the rule must be right about. `trackpadScroll.test.ts` fails on `wheelDeltaY`/`wheelDeltaX`/`deltaMode` reappearing in NodeEditor: do not reintroduce a sniff without ground truth measured on a real mouse AND a real trackpad. The flag DEFAULTS to the mouse model — historical behaviour, and a wrong default must cost the recoverable thing. It is read with `getState()` INSIDE the wheel handler, never closed over: the listener is bound once, so a captured value would freeze the setting at mount and the toggle would read as doing nothing until a reload.
  - The toolbar's right-click popup (`.toolbar__prefs-popover`) is that toggle's home, holds the two optional-category switches beside it (Textures, Distance fields — see that convention) and the GLB import's “Allow more than 10 materials” override (hidden in a study session; its hint is its OWN constant, since `optionalCategories.test.ts` counts `OPTIONAL_CATEGORY_HINTS`, and the popover's height is computed from the row count inside the component because that row comes and goes), and is the natural place for any later app-wide preference. It is a plain LIST with no heading (the "Input settings" title went on 2026-09-04 when the library rows arrived: a heading over three checkboxes was a row that did nothing — and its `aria-label` is "Settings" now, since a name that fits only the first row misnames the other two to a screen reader), ONE line per setting — the explanation is the row's `title`, raised by the app-wide `TooltipLayer`, the same "a list you scan, the detail on hover" rule the Add-node menu follows. The title sits on the LABEL, which wraps both the box and its text, so either half raises it (TooltipLayer resolves its host with `closest('[title]')`). It refuses to open on a text input — a field's context menu is the one place users genuinely rely on the OS one — and on the EXPORT / Download-app / ☰ controls, which own right-click for their own popovers; EXPORT's handler `preventDefault`s but does NOT `stopPropagation`, so without that guard both popovers opened at once. **It also opens on a touch LONG-PRESS anywhere on the bar** (`useLongPress(barRef, …)`, 2026-09-04 — iPadOS dispatches no `contextmenu` from a sustained touch, and the list is the ONLY way to switch the optional categories on), through the SAME guard (`prefsClaimedElsewhere`, module scope) so the two gestures cannot disagree about who owns a spot on the bar. Untested on a real iPad.
  - **One finger on touch never navigates** — it drags nodes and edges, draws, and marquee-selects, which is why `panOnDrag` and `selectionOnDrag` are both OFF on coarse pointers (`isCoarsePointer`). With React Flow's drag-to-pan on, the FIRST finger of a two-finger pan lands on the pane and opens a rubber band instead, so an empty-canvas two-finger pan came out as a selection box; dropping both leaves the touch-nav effect unopposed. The desktop model is untouched by any of this.
  - **Escape hatches that must keep working**: `Ctrl`/`Cmd`+wheel zooms on every device, and double-tap-and-drag pans on a trackpad, where the natural "grab" gesture is tap-tap-hold-drag and `panOnDrag={[1, 2]}` gives it no button.

- **A canvas gesture takes the preview iframe out of hit-testing** (`:root.fs-canvas-busy .shader-preview__iframe { pointer-events: none }`, armed by NodeEditor's `setCanvasBusy`). The preview is a SANDBOXED, opaque-origin document, so the instant the pointer crosses into it the parent stops receiving mouse events at all — including the `mouseup` d3-zoom listens for on the window. A middle-button pan dragged over the preview therefore **never ended**: release the button there and the canvas kept panning, because as far as the parent knew the button was still down (reproduced, and re-reproduced as a negative control by disabling just this rule). Node drags and connection drags cross the same boundary and hang the same way, so all three bracket it: `onMoveStart`/`onMoveEnd`, `onNodeDragStart`/`onNodeDragStop`, `onConnectStart`/`onConnectEnd`. The counter is REFCOUNTED because those overlap — React Flow fires `onMoveStart` for the pan under a selection drag — and a boolean would let whichever ended first re-arm the iframe under the one still running. Each release sits ABOVE the early returns in its handler (the drag-chrome rule), and NodeEditor's unmount clears the class outright: a stranded flag leaves the 3D view permanently unclickable. At rest the iframe keeps every event, so orbiting is untouched. **A CONNECTION drag also arms the app-wide selection chrome** (`setConnectChrome` beside it, over `beginDragChrome(null)` — utils/dragChrome.ts, the same refcounted `.fs-dragging` every seam grip uses; `null` because React Flow owns the pointer's look during a connect and a pinned `move` would fight the connection line). A wire drag sweeps the pointer across the whole window, and the engines disagree about whether the PRESSED element's `user-select: none` protects the gesture: MEASURED 2026-09-18, one wire dragged off a socket painted **38 selection rectangles** over the nodes and chrome in WebKit 26.5 and NONE in Chrome 152, while a grip drag — which already armed this — painted none in either. That was the owner's report ("when edges are dragged out the strange browser selections (partial) highlight appears"), from macOS, and Chrome's clean result is an accident of where the text happens to sit rather than a guarantee. A node drag and the marquee were measured CLEAN in both engines and are deliberately left alone. The token is MODULE scope beside `canvasBusy`, because the release has to be reachable from the pointer reaper AND from unmount, neither of which can see a ref; the reaper releases it ABOVE its `canvasBusy === 0` early return, since the two flags are cleared by different handlers and a stranded `.fs-dragging` makes the whole app unselectable. `connectionDragChrome.test.ts` pins all four sites.
- **Preview renderer backend (WebGPU vs WebGL2)**: the sandboxed preview iframe (`tslToPreviewHTML.ts`) and the standalone podest viewer (`public/podest.html`) both inject `<a-scene>` only AFTER a WebGPU pre-flight — three r184 auto-selects WebGPU on `navigator.gpu != null` ALONE, so the pre-flight requests a real adapter (2s timeout) and hides `navigator.gpu` (→ WebGL2 via GLSLNodeBuilder, TSL compiles identically) when it can't deliver. **Safari is force-hidden to WebGL2 up front** (the UA branch inside the emitted `__fsForceWebGL2`) — three r184's WebGPU backend can be handed an adapter yet never paint on Safari (flat-color pane, no error). A 6s watchdog turns any remaining no-paint into a visible sticky error. **The XR popups reach the same backend a DIFFERENT way** (2026-08-31): they force it declaratively with `<a-scene renderer="backend: webgl">` and run no pre-flight at all, since the decision is already made — the editor's XR popup, podest's XR popup and the A-Frame tab page all do this. The SANDBOXED preview deliberately keeps `hideGpu()`: its gpu-hiding is not about VR (it serves Safari-no-paint, adapter-less WebGPU and the WGSL/GLSL toggle), and the hidden-gpu state IS the truth that the `fs:backend` report, the watchdog wording and `AUTO_IS_WEBGL2` read — swap it for the attribute and that label silently lies.
- **Podest unattended / pedestal mode** (`public/podest.html`): the viewer is meant to run for WEEKS with nobody touching it, which is a different problem from "a page that works". The presentation layer is the ⛶/⤡ fullscreen button (bottom-right) plus an optional top-centre title (auto-derived from the dropped file name, `_`→space, click it to edit text + colour; `textContent` only). **Presentation mode is deliberately WIDER than the Fullscreen API**: fullscreen needs a user gesture, and an unattended pedestal has nobody to give one after a browser auto-update — it would come back windowed with the panel over the artwork and, far worse, no wake lock, so the display blanks at the OS idle timeout and stays black for the week. So the mode is remembered (`fs:viewerPresenting`, or `?present=1` for a kiosk launcher) and `is-present` on `<html>` — not `is-fs` — is what hides the panel, hint and error banner; `is-fs` governs only the ⛶ button's own auto-fade, since in windowed presentation that button is the way back in (⛶ then Esc leaves the remembered mode). Everything that must survive the reload asks `presenting()`, never `fsElement()` — **including the release-race guard inside `requestWakeLock`**, which gated on fullscreen would release every lock the instant its promise resolved and silently undo the whole thing. Behind that sit five long-run guards, each fixing something that actually breaks on that timescale: (1) **the stage iframe is disposable** — `applyStateToStage()` replays background/spin/geometry/shader on every `fs:ready`, so the pending queue is DISCARDED rather than flushed (replaying both would recompile the shader twice) and any restart is invisible; (2) **`time` is folded back every ~4.4 h** — three's `time` is a float32 accumulating seconds since boot, so its resolution decays to 7.8 ms after a day, 62 ms after a week and 250 ms after a month (a slideshow); the stage takes `renderer._nodes.nodeFrame.time %= TIME_WRAP` where `TIME_WRAP` is an exact multiple of 2π (2π × 2520 ≈ 15833 s), so `sin(time)`/`cos(time)` cross the fold with NO visible jump and the quantum never exceeds ~1 ms (a shader's own `time.mul(20)` multiplies it) — modulo not zeroing, so the fold stays 2π-aligned however far behind the beat has fallen; if that private path ever disappears the beat reports `wrapNeeded` (rebasing as it fires, so it can't latch) and the parent restarts the stage instead. The stage also rebases `nodeFrame.lastTime` on `visibilitychange`→visible, or the first frame after an overnight sleep would dump the whole hidden gap into `time` in one step; (3) **device loss is terminal and silent** — the stage wraps `renderer.onDeviceLost` (keeping three's own handler) AND hooks the raw `backend.device.lost` promise, because three returns early on `reason === 'destroyed'` and would never call the callback, plus a `webglcontextlost` listener; all report `fs:stage-dead`, and a 5 s `fs:beat` carrying the cumulative `info.render.calls` lets the parent catch a stall with no throw at all (best-effort: it proves the loop ran, not that pixels landed); restarts are floored at 30 s apart and after 6 in a row back off to one retry every 10 min — never a permanent give-up, since a transient driver fault must not end the exhibit (watchdog is suspended while the document is hidden — a frozen counter there is normal); (4) **the last drop is mirrored to IndexedDB** (`fs-podest`/`session`, throw-safe, shader ≤24 M chars + model ≤64 MB — the model cap is the editor's `MESH_MAX_BYTES`, deliberately NOT the 96 MB zip cap; a payload over either is left out of the mirror (and when nothing is left to mirror both records are DELETED, so the pedestal restarts empty), so a user load of one — drop, picker, a manual work-folder pick, or re-ticking auto-restore — says so in the banner while the panel is up (`noteRestoreLimits`/`restoreLimitText`: never in presentation mode, never on a cycle tick, never with auto-restore off, never from the boot restore, and only for the part THIS load changed; called after BOTH halves of a drop land, because `loadShaderText` clears the banner)) and replayed at boot, because a browser auto-update or power blip otherwise leaves a grey sphere — the record carries the tuned **uniform values** too (losing the exhibitor's tuning shows the wrong artwork), a drop that lands first always wins, and a user-typed title outranks the replayed one; (5) **the screen wake lock** is requested at boot, on entering presentation mode, on `visibilitychange`, and again on every 5 s watchdog tick — the platform can drop a lock on its own and a UA may refuse it until it can grant it, so the only robust pattern is to keep asking (it early-returns when a lock is already held). Also: the error relay is rate-limited IN the sandbox (identical repeats/minute + 20/minute cap) since a per-frame `console.error` would otherwise postMessage forever, banners self-dismiss after 20 s, the zip reader is a hand-written twin of `utils/zipReader.ts` whose three caps (512 entries, 96 MB summed — `MESH_MAX_BYTES` + 32 MB, so a bundle the editor exports reopens — 512-byte names) `zipReader.test.ts` pins against the editor's, and a cap failure is a sentence rather than a parser error ('Could not open the zip: it unpacks to more than 96 MB.' / '… holds more than 512 files.', `zipLimitError`/`zipErrorText`, executed by `podestLimits.test.ts`) while every other failure keeps 'Could not read zip: …', and 90 s of idle in fullscreen returns the camera to the framing pinned by `fs:save-view` at entry (a visitor's spin must not outlive them). **Checked and deliberately NOT fixed**: the bundle's anime.js calls the undeclared `activeInstance[i].pause()` on visibilitychange, but A-Frame builds its instances with `autoplay:false` and ticks them by hand, so anime's active list is always empty and that line never runs (verified in a browser).
- **Podest VR locomotion (`vr-nav`, in `buildXrDoc`'s popup document only)**: the immersive popup used to drop the visitor at the reference-space origin, which is exactly where the artwork is — `fit-bounds` normalizes every mesh to 1.6 units, so a headset opened on the INSIDE of the sphere. Raising the piece to eye height (the old `EYE` fix) addressed the wrong axis. Moving the ART cannot fix it either: `local-floor`'s yaw comes from the guardian, not from where the wearer is looking, so "in front" is unknowable until a pose arrives. `vr-nav` therefore steps the **camera rig** back `NAV_HOME` metres along the head's own forward on the first tracked frame. Load-bearing details, each a way this silently goes wrong. **The rig works because three composes the XR pose with the camera's PARENT world matrix** (`WebXRManager.updateUserCamera`) — moving the camera entity itself does nothing, the pose overwrites it every frame. **The head is read from the FRAME (`getViewerPose`), never from the camera object3D**: A-Frame ticks before it renders, so on a session's first tick the camera still carries its flat orbit-controls transform (`0 1.4 7`) and an entry framing measured off it lands metres away. The rig returns to the origin on `exit-vr`, which is what keeps the flat orbit view identical to before. **Input is read straight off `session.inputSources` + `targetRaySpace`**, not through A-Frame's controller components — `laser-controls`/`oculus-touch-controls` fetch their models from `cdn.aframe.io` and this page must run offline. **The HANDS are drawn by `vr-nav` itself** (joint beads on an InstancedMesh parented to the rig, from the same `fillPoses` output the gesture reads) and NOT by `hand-tracking-controls`: that component adds a `wristObject3D` to the **scene root** and registers a `child-attached` handler that reparents every child entity onto it — including the 25 joint dots it creates itself — so its hands render at `wristPose × jointPose` in scene-root space, bypassing the camera rig entirely (they ignore the entry framing and every teleport) and carrying the wrist transform twice. MEASURED ON A HEADSET as hands sitting metres from the real ones. Hands drawn from the same poses under the same rig as the head cannot drift from it; `podestVrNav.test.ts` asserts the mesh's parent IS the rig. Losing the component also means the scene's own `webxr="optionalFeatures: hand-tracking"` is now the only thing requesting the feature. Two triggers share one aim→commit path: `select` (controller trigger, or the runtime's hand pinch — press to aim, release to go) and a **Quest-style fist-with-thumb button classified here from the 25 joint poses**, because Meta's microgesture recognizer is an OpenXR extension WebXR does not expose. Its thresholds are fractions of the wearer's own hand (wrist→middle-metacarpal, ~9 cm), with a 4-frame arm and press/release hysteresis — the thumb is self-occluded by the fist at exactly the moment it is pressed, so tracking confidence is worst where the decision is made. **The button is the thumb tip's HEIGHT over the index knuckle, never its distance from it.** The first build used distance (press < 0.55, release > 0.85 of hand scale) and a thumbs-up fist sits inside that band: the button latched pressed on the very frame it armed, `commit()` no-op'd because `valid` is only computed later in `aim()`, and it could never release — so nothing teleported again, and the pinch path died with it since a fist-owned hand ignores `select`. Two rules came out of that: a press may not land on the arming frame (`st.armed > 1`), and RELEASE must sit BELOW ARM so re-arming cannot re-trigger. The unit tests did not catch it because they validated the classifier against a synthetic hand invented alongside it — they proved the code matched the assumption, not the anatomy. A tight fist can also cross the runtime's own pinch threshold, so a hand the fist detector owns ignores `select` outright. **The 30-unit bound is an annulus, not a nav mesh** — the scene has no floor geometry to walk on (an artwork on a background colour is all there is), so a mesh would have to be invented before it could be raycast; targets are clamped into `NAV_INNER`…`NAV_RADIUS` (the inner ring is what stops a visitor teleporting inside the piece). **A clamp is never a refusal**: aiming past the edge lands you at the edge, and the marker is drawn where you will ACTUALLY arrive — a marker that vanishes reads as a broken button, which is how an earlier 60 m ray cap put a dead zone exactly where "aim at the far edge" lives. The aim is shown as a **ring plus a bead arc** — a quadratic Bézier LOFTED above the straight aim, deliberately not a ballistic curve: targeting stays the straight ray so the whole ring remains reachable (a projectile caps range at v²/g and would silently make the outer bound unaimable), and the arc's only contract is that it ends on the marker, which it does by construction. Beads on an InstancedMesh rather than a `THREE.Line` because line width is capped at one pixel on every backend, and a one-pixel thread across a headset's field of view is not a visible control. The jump moves the **head** onto the marker, not the rig origin (someone who physically walked two steps would otherwise arrive two steps off), under a blink so the translation is never seen, and **physical walking is never counter-moved** — pulling the rig back under someone's feet is how you make them sick, so the bound leashes teleports only. Unattended recovery: 90 s of a MOTIONLESS head returns the visitor home (a worn headset is never still that long, so it can only fire when nobody is wearing it), skipped when the return is under 35 cm so an empty room doesn't blink every 90 s. All of this lives in the popup document, so it cannot touch the pedestal's own long-run loop. `src/podestVrNav.test.ts` runs the generated component against a real `three` and synthetic XR frames (entry framing from arbitrary yaw, the gesture state machine, the annulus, the idle return) — **but nothing here has run on a headset**; the gesture thresholds in particular are derived, not measured, and Horizon OS may claim the same hand pose for its own navigation. The editor's XR popup (`tslToPreviewHTML` xr mode) still centres the visitor on the artwork and has none of this.
- **XR popup (preview "VR" button)**: opens the shader at the app's REAL origin (WebXR needs a top-level page; hidden on desktop builds). `tslToPreviewHTML`'s `xr` mode now (1) **auto-enters immersive VR** once `navigator.xr.isSessionSupported('immersive-vr')` confirms real support — never on the magic-window fallback, which would strand the user in pseudo-VR — while keeping an in-document `#vr-gate` button visible until a session actually starts (a `window.open`+`document.write` document may not inherit the opening click's transient activation, and an in-document gesture is what Quest Browser reliably accepts); on non-XR devices the same gate becomes a Fullscreen toggle (capability-resolved label, never a UA sniff); and (2) draws a head-locked frame-time/FPS panel (`fs-xr-stats`): a CanvasTexture quad added to the THREE camera OBJECT via `getObject3D('camera')` — a child `<a-entity>` would world-lock in XR — redrawn at ~4Hz so the readout doesn't bias the GPU cost it reports, rasterized to canvas because A-Frame's `<a-text>` fetches its MSDF font from a CDN (this app must run offline). The number is the presented frame PERIOD (includes vsync — reads 72/90/120Hz until the shader actually misses frames). It never runs a module found INSIDE a model: every preview copy is payload-free by construction (`createPreviewMesh`, and the popup's blob is `previewMesh.bytes`), no document the app itself runs spells loader 0.8's opt-in (the GLB-mode module header does, on purpose, for recipients), and the popup closes the loader's one-way latch (`embeddedModuleNeverRuns.test.ts` pins all three and runs the real vendored loader). A module-only RESTORE is a different thing: it goes through the bare-script path exactly like an imported `.js`, so the popup may run what the user chose to restore, never what merely rode in the model bytes.
- **Edge interactions** (`TypedEdge` + `bezierGeometry.ts` + `NodeEditor` handlers): edges are drag-to-disconnect, and support (1) **drop-on-edge insertion** — dragging a node/tile within `DROP_ON_EDGE_RADIUS` (screen px ÷ zoom) of an edge splices it in (`findNearestEdge`/`tryInsertOnEdge`), and (2) **routing waypoints** — double-click an edge or right-click → "Add routing point" drops a draggable point the wire curves through (Catmull-Rom spline); double-click a point removes it. Waypoints live on `edge.data.waypoints` (flow coords), are **visual-only** (graphToCode/cpuEvaluator never read them; persist via localStorage autosave + undo, and are carried across a code→graph resync by endpoint match in `useSyncEngine` — like groups. They also ROUND-TRIP through a `.js`/zip export→import: `buildProjectState` embeds whole edge objects (`exportShader.ts`) and `applyProjectToStore` restores them (`projectImport.ts`), so only a BARE-SCRIPT import — one with no FASTSHADERS_PROJECT_V1 block — loses them). **`edge.data` is sanitized as adversarial input on ALL THREE restore paths** (`utils/edgeExtras.ts` — `sanitizeEdgeExtras`, called from `loadGraph`, `applyProjectToStore`, and `loadSavedGroups` — the saved-group library is the same localStorage trust level and instantiating a group clones its edges straight into the graph): it is the one graph payload the engine never reads, which is exactly why it was unvalidated, while `TypedEdge` dereferences `w.x` during RENDER with no error boundary anywhere — `waypoints: [null]` (or a string, whose `.length` passes the `hasWaypoints` gate) threw out of render, blanked the page, and the 300 ms autosave then persisted the poison so every reload blanked again. The sanitizer drops non-object edge ELEMENTS, whitelists `data` to `dataType`/`waypoints`, clamps coords to ±1e6, caps at `MAX_WAYPOINTS_PER_EDGE` (64), and returns the SAME array when nothing needed changing — the autosave subscriber and `selectionOnlyGraphChange` compare edges by `data` reference. **The renderer and the drop-on-edge hit test share `bezierGeometry.ts`** so what highlights is exactly what snaps — a routed edge is measured against the SAME spline it draws (`distancePointToSpline`). `setEdgeWaypoints` pushes history once per gesture (add/remove/drag-start), never per drag-move frame. **A splice lands on the node's first FREE input, not always its first** (`edgeSplice.ts`, pure + tested): dragging an already-wired node onto a new edge used to delete whatever fed `inputs[0]`, so a half-built Multiply lost the operand you had just given it. Candidates come from `effectiveInputs(def, connected, /*trailing empty*/ true, valued)` — so a variadic fold GROWS a fresh socket instead of reporting itself full (`append` stops when its vec4 is full — CHANNELS, not sockets; see `utils/appendCapacity.ts`), and hidden `exposedPorts` params (Image's `uv`, Mic's `gain`) stay eligible in plain registry order with `tryInsertOnEdge` calling `exposeConnectedTarget` so the new edge can't point at a socket that never mounts. Only when EVERY socket is taken does it fall back to `inputs[0]` and replace that edge — the historical behaviour. Splice-time cycle risk is unchanged by this (reachability out of the node depends on its OUTGOING edges, and the port the splice picks is incoming).
- **The edge menu's "Insert node" puts a node ON the wire** (`components/NodeEditor/edgeInsert.ts`; right-click an edge → Insert node → the search list opens at the same point and whatever is chosen is spliced in). It is the SAME splice a node or tile dropped near a wire performs — `spliceNodeIntoEdge` was extracted out of NodeEditor's `tryInsertOnEdge`, which is now only the SEARCH half (`findNearestEdge` then the shared splice), so a wire spliced by pointing at it and one spliced by picking from a list cannot land on different ports under different rules. Three things are load-bearing. The edge rides in the context menu's EXISTING `edgeId` field rather than a tenth positional argument to `openContextMenu` — the field already means "the edge this menu is about" — and AddNodeMenu reads it as `spliceEdgeId`. The splice runs INSIDE `handleAddNode`'s `asOneHistoryEntry`, so the node and both wires are one undo step (the rule the palette-tile path states: one Cmd+Z must not leave a node standing spliced-out mid-graph). And **a def with no outputs is refused by the splice itself**, not at the call site: both sinks declare `outputs: []`, so `def.outputs[0].id` is a TypeError on the node type a user is most likely to reach for while pointing at a wire — the drag paths happen to guard it in their callers (`def.inputs.length > 0 && def.outputs.length > 0`), which is exactly the kind of guard a new caller forgets. Refused means the node is still ADDED where the menu was opened, just not wired. `exposeConnectedTarget` moved into the same module (it is the store-touching half of landing a connection, and the splice needs it); `edgeSplice.ts` keeps the pure port CHOICE. `edgeInsert.test.ts` drives the real store for the rewiring, the first-free-input rule and both refusals, and source-pins all three surfaces.
- **A wire dropped on empty canvas connects from EITHER end** (`components/NodeEditor/edgeInsert.ts` — `DroppedPin`, `connectDroppedWire`). Releasing a connection over nothing opens the AddNodeMenu with the pin that started it, and picking a node wires the two: pull from an OUTPUT and the new node is fed by that pin (its `inputs[0]`), pull from an INPUT and the new node feeds it (its `outputs[0]`). Only the output half worked, and the input half was not merely unimplemented — `onConnectStart` discarded the pin outright for a `target` handle, three functions before anything could have used it, so half the gesture opened a menu and then added a loose unwired node. **The side rides in the menu state as `sourceHandleType`, React Flow's own `handleType` vocabulary carried verbatim** so there is nothing to translate; it is not derived from the registry, because a collapsed group's synthetic boundary sockets match no registry port. Getting the direction wrong is the failure to fear here: an edge authored OUT of an input renders as NOTHING while the store keeps it and codegen still reads it. `ConnectionStub` takes the same field — React Flow keeps the two sides in separate `handleBounds` buckets and each side has its own port list for the wire's colour, so reading `source` for an input pin finds nothing and the stub silently does not draw — and mirrors its control points, or a wire dropped left of an input doubles back through its own node. **The menu's connect also stopped being a fourth connect path**: `connectNodes` is `applyConnection`'s core (single-input replacement + `exposeConnectedTarget`) extracted so both obey one rule — the menu restated neither, so it could double-feed an occupied input and point an edge at a hidden parameter socket that never mounts. The telemetry and the image→normal colour-space flip stay with `applyConnection`: they need its node scan, and the flip is unreachable from the menu today (an Image node added from the menu is EMPTY since GLB Phase 4; the texture picker can now fill one, so a wire dropped from the Output's Normal onto a menu-added Image node that is later filled connects through `connectNodes` WITHOUT the colour-space flip — a known follow-up).
