(1) **It is an ADD-SURFACE filter, never a registry filter** — the `editorVisibility` rule verbatim, and a SECOND layer above it: `editorVisibility.json` hides individual UNFINISHED nodes as project source (the same answer for every user of a build), this hides whole FINISHED categories per browser — plus each family's COMPANIONS, node types that live in another category but exist for the family (`OPTIONAL_CATEGORY_COMPANIONS`: Distance fields takes the Raymarch Output and Ray Direction with it; `withheldNodeTypes` is applied INSIDE `getEditorDefinitions(hidden)` beside the category test, so every consumer of the narrowed set gets both without a second call). `getEditorDefinitions()` is still the FILE's answer — what `nodeSearch.test.ts`/`nodeLabelRename.test.ts` rank over, so flipping a switch cannot redden them — and `getEditorDefinitions(hidden)` / `searchNodes(query, hidden)` narrow it by the set `hiddenOptionalCategories(flags)` returns, memoized per set so the identity rule holds there too (a consumer builds a fresh `Set` per render and must get the SAME array back). **An add surface passes the set on EVERY call** — `optionalCategories.test.ts` fails on a bare `getEditorDefinitions()` or a one-argument `searchNodes()` anywhere under `components/NodeEditor/`, because the default is the family walking back in through the search box. (2) **Two gate sites in the content browser, not one** — three, counting the measurement: the tab strip renders `visibleCategories` (= the pure `visibleTabs(displayCategories, flags)`), but a live SEARCH appends texture cards to the generic strip on EVERY tab, so `filteredTextures` returns `[]` while Textures is off, ABOVE its lazy build — a session that never switches the library on never pays the ~84 ms texture parse. And the tallest-tile high-water mark effect takes the flags as a dep: a family switched on lands its tiles in the All strip with NO tab change, and without the dep the strip never re-measures and a taller newcomer is clipped. (3) **A stored `fs:assetTab` naming a switched-off category stays VALID and is DERIVED to `'all'` at render (`effectiveTab`, pure), never written back**: `displayCategories`, `BROWSER_TABS` and the validator stay module-scope (usePersistedState needs stable identities) and list every tab the BUILD allows; the user's switches are applied per render. Writing `'all'` back would discard the user's tab for good (usePersistedState persists every value); deriving it means the tab returns the moment the switch does. (4) **Nothing under `engine/`, `utils/` or `hooks/` may import the module** (the store is the one exception — it holds the flag), so a `.fastshader` holding those nodes loads, renders, compiles and exports byte-identically whether the switch is on or off; `instantiateBuiltinTexture` is ungated, and node-editor.html + the Node Designer keep seeing everything. (5) **The study clean slate resets both** (`cleanSlateForStudy` removes the keys and restores `DEFAULT_OPTIONAL_CATEGORIES`): what the palette offers is part of the condition every participant must meet identically, so the previous user's switches are dropped with the rest of their state. The DESKTOP build follows the same default — the old desktop-unlocked exception existed only because `/textures` was unreachable there, and the switch is not. The rows are labelled with the category's own tab name (`formatCategoryLabel`, Latvian-aware) so the switch and the tab it summons read as one thing, and each hint is the row's `title`.
- **"Hard reload" is the reload button's right-click menu** (`utils/hardReload.ts` + the `.toolbar__local toolbar__reload-wrap` wrapper in Toolbar.tsx). Right-click — or a sustained press on touch, where there is no second button — opens two rows, Reload and Hard reload. It exists because the desktop shell has no browser chrome at all and an iPad's has no hard-reload gesture. **What it actually does is navigate to `?fsreload=<stamp>`**, which `main.tsx` then strips from the address bar with `replaceState` so the parameter isn't carried into every bookmark: a different document URL is a different HTTP cache entry, so index.html is refetched, and since Vite content-hashes the JS/CSS it references that is exactly how a newly deployed build gets picked up. The unhashed `public/` assets (the A-Frame bundle, the shaderloader, the `.obj` models) keep their cached copies — the honest limit, and the tooltip says so. Four traps, all pinned by `hardReload.test.ts`: `location.reload(true)` does NOTHING (the Location IDL takes no arguments; TS declares `reload(): void`, so the compile error tends to get cast away and ship a right-click identical to the left-click); the cleared-keys list is EXPLICIT and names no eval key, because a `sessionStorage.clear()` there would drop a participant's telemetry journal and return them to the consent screen mid-study; the wrapper must stay inside `openPrefs`' `closest()` guard (APPEND only — `trackpadScroll.test.ts` anchors that string at its opening quote) or the trackpad-prefs popover opens on top of it while the bar is expanded, and only then, since `.toolbar__overflow` is already guarded; and the long-press binds to the ELEMENT held in state, never a ref, because React remounts this button into the ☰ menu when the toolbar collapses and `useLongPress` keys on the target's identity — a ref object's identity never changes, so the gesture would die silently on the first collapse (the hook was widened to accept either). The wrapper reuses `.toolbar__local` for its `position: relative`, the ☰ re-anchoring rule and the dark-theme shadow, which is an explicit selector list a bespoke class would miss.
- **Asset tiles are spaced by SILHOUETTE, not by frame** (`components/NodeEditor/tileSilhouette.ts`, 2026-09-11, owner: "the spacing in assets between nodes is not even"). The frames were already evenly spaced (12.2–12.9 px, measured), but the eye reads the gap from the outermost thing drawn, and sockets straddle the frame: a node with inputs has dots out on its left, the colour swatch's perimeter socket is 1.5× (`CARD_COLOR_SCALE`), the Output's preview socket is twice a socket — so the visible gaps ran 3.5–10.3 px. ContentBrowser's tile-measurement rAF now runs `applyTileSilhouettes(strip)`: read every tile's frame and socket rects, THEN write `--fs-sock-l`/`-r` on each (one layout, not one per tile), which `.node-preview-card`'s padding adds to its 8px. Measured rather than tabled per node type, because the overhang depends on the socket token, the cost scale, the swatch counter-scale and each node's own insets, and a tile added tomorrow would be missing from a table. The values are in the tile's OWN px (screen ÷ rendered scale), so they are zoom-independent and dragging the bar's top edge never re-measures them. The node-editor.html overview draws the same cards without the pass, so its tiles keep the plain 8px.
- **Asset-browser ordering**: node tiles sort by GPU points (cheapest first, every tab — not just Noise). **Presets sort by GPU points too** (`byCost`, 2026-08-27), on the SAME `totalCost` their tile's `AssetCostBadge` prints — so the strip reads as a price ladder (4 for gradient up to 76 for studio-shine), which is the point of a library whose pedagogy is that a tier-3 look need not be expensive. Deliberately NOT `nodeCostPoints`: that re-derives a variadic fold as base × (N−1) and disagrees with the badge by a point or two (studio-shine 78 vs 76), and a sort contradicting the number on screen reads as a bug. **Textures sort by GPU points too** (2026-08-29): they already PRINT `totalCost` on their tiles, and a strip ordered by something its own tiles do not show reads as unordered. They used to sort by member count on the reasoning that a texture is a graph you open and read — but node count turns out to be a poor proxy for the price anyway (Static Noise is the smallest texture at 16 nodes and the third most expensive of the cheap half), and two ready-made strips sorted by two different rules was the thing actually causing confusion. One ladder now: Grid 22 → Crumpled Fabric 510. `bySize` is gone, and `noUnusedLocals` is what would have caught it lingering. Consequence to accept: the Presets strip is no longer the easy→advanced ladder — cost orders ACROSS tiers (tier-2 Vertex Wave at 8 sits third; tier-3 Teleport Beam at 29 precedes tier-1 Stripes at 30), so the tier now lives only in each preset's blurb and in-frame note. Both are display-layer sorts in `ContentBrowser` — `builtinPresets.ts` keeps its tier order (and the test that pins it), and `builtinPresets.test.ts` source-pins that BOTH strips use `byCost`, that `byCost` reads the same `totalCost` the badge draws, that `bySize` is gone, and that every preset AND texture costs > 0, since a zero-cost asset would clump at the head with no badge to explain it.
- **Time node speed**: the Time node carries a `speed` multiplier in `defaultValues`, shown ON the node as an always-visible editable `× <n>` row (`.clock-node__speed-row`) and, when the `speed` socket is wired, as the value ARRIVING through the shared `edgeValueLabel` — plain text, no plate, exactly like the Sound node's wired params. It was previously a read-only red chip in the bottom-right corner that rendered ONLY when `speed !== 1`, so at the default the node carried no sign it had a multiplier at all, which reads as the control having gone missing rather than as "it is at 1". Also settings-editable through the generic `NodeSettingsMenu` defaultValues loop. **Its sockets live INSIDE `.clock-node__canvas-wrap`** so they centre on the clock FACE: positioned against the whole node they sat exactly half a header-height too high, and moved again whenever a long varName wrapped the title to a second line. The left/right insets must always be overridden as a PAIR — only `-right` was, so the shared `transform: translateY(-50%)` override (which also strips React Flow's own `translateX(-50%)`) left the speed socket floating ~6px INSIDE the card while the output sat on the edge. `graphToCode` has a dedicated `def.type === 'time'` branch placed BEFORE the generic `inputs.length === 0 && defaultValues` branch (which would emit the invalid `time(1)` — `time` is a uniform node OBJECT, not a callable): `speed === 1`, a missing key, or any non-finite value emits the historical bare `const time1 = time;` BYTE-FOR-BYTE, so no already-exported shader changes; otherwise it emits the method chain `const time1 = time.mul(k);` (no extra import, same convention as the noise `scale` param). `codeToGraph`'s `tryParseTimeSpeed` collapses that shape back — but ONLY when the receiver is the bare `time` identifier, `time` is unshadowed, there is exactly one argument, and it folds to a numeric constant. It deliberately does NOT collapse `mul(time, k)`, `mul(time1, k)` (the human idiom — ~51 occurrences across the `Tests/` corpus, usually two muls sharing one Time node) or a non-literal multiplier (`mul(t, speed)` in builtinTextures/builtinPresets). **`ensureBareInputNode` needs its `def.defaultValues` bail-out to except `time`** — without it, adding defaultValues silently drops the Time node AND its edge from `sin(time)`/`add(pos, time, normal)` with no error. `values.speed` is adversarial at all three read sites (graphToCode, cpuEvaluator, ClockNode): `Number()` then `Number.isFinite() ? … : 1`, never `String()` and never `resolveExposedParam`. ClockNode integrates `dt * speed` into a phase accumulator (never `Date.now() * speed`, which teleports the hand on every scrub frame) with the rAF deps kept `[]`. `complexity.json` keeps `"time": 0` — `nodeCostPoints` can only express value-dependent cost for `chainable` defs, and `node.data.cost` is a creation-time snapshot frozen into saved graphs.
- **Drop-time image conversion is ASKED, not assumed** (`Modals/ImageImportModal`): dropping image(s) opens one dialog per drop — a single line, "Convert image to optimized format?", **Yes** (green) / **No**, plus "Don't ask again" persisting to `fs:imageConvert` (`imageConvertMode: 'ask'|'always'|'never'`). **That preference MUST stay reversible from the UI** — it is offered in the Image node's settings menu ("Optimize on import": Ask each time / Always / Never) and in the "?" panel, and a remembered `never` announces itself on every drop via the one-liner. Without those, ticking the box next to "No" silently pins every future drop with no way back and no signal, which reads exactly like the feature being broken. **No is not Cancel** — the image imports either way, so Escape and the backdrop mean No; the explanation lives in the node's settings menu (Format / Resolution / Size / Original) rather than in a dialog standing between a drag and the canvas. It is a question because the conversion is lossy in a way the app cannot judge: a normal map wants "keep", a photo wants "convert", and at drop time the app does not know which it has (the Data-map flag is only set afterwards). "Keep as-is" still runs the canvas round-trip — EXIF/GPS strip, device texture cap, payload budget are not preferences — it only turns off the FORMAT change (`chooseFormat`'s `allowWebp: false` keeps the image in its own family) and the power-of-two snap. When the user asked to convert and **this browser cannot encode WebP** — MEASURED on this machine 2026-08-02: Safari 26.5.2 *decodes* WebP fine (`<img>` and `createImageBitmap`) but `toBlob`/`toDataURL('image/webp')` still return `image/png`, while Chrome 150 encodes it and q=1 really is lossless — the canvas shows a top-centre ONE-LINER ("Not optimized — stored as JPEG") with a **?** opening `Modals/ImageConvertInfoModal` and a dismiss; it self-clears after 12s and can be turned off for good from that panel (`hideImageConvertNotice`, `fs:hideImageConvertNotice`). The one-liner is STORE state (`importNote` + `showImportNote`/`dismissImportNote`, holding LINES that `utils/importNote.ts` renders; session-only — never history, the autosave or localStorage) so that any surface can post to it, not only the canvas drop; a later note REPLACES the earlier one, and the timer and ✕ dismiss BY ID so an older note's timer cannot clear a newer note. That is also what lets the store's "Add anyway" re-encode report. A drop's note carries the per-image budget lines (N5) above the WebP line; `hideImageConvertNotice` silences only the WebP-capability line and its "?" (which renders only beside it), because a budget line is a cap and caps are not hideable. Not a modal, deliberately: it reports a browser capability, not a decision, so it must not stand between a drag and the canvas — but a capability limit the user can't see at all is worse than the fallback itself, which is how a dropped `.png` silently became a JPEG (PNG blew the 600 K budget at 1024×2048, leaving JPEG as the only candidate). The Image node's card label also shows the **stored** extension, not the dropped file's (`displayImageFileName`, pure + tested) — a re-encode routinely leaves `.png` on WebP bytes, and the card must not assert a format the node doesn't hold; the `title` keeps the name as dropped. **The GLB import builder is the recorded EXCEPTION to this question** (`engine/gltfImport.ts` + `utils/gltfTextureEncode.ts`, GLB Phase 5 Step 8): textures extracted from a dropped model are optimised automatically, without the dialog, and a remembered `never` does not apply — the answer is made by SLOT (`slotEncodeClass` over `glbSlotPolicy`, owner rule Q12): base colour and emissive lossy at up to 1024 px even from a PNG; a normal or metallic-roughness map at up to 512 px, lossless-ONLY from a lossless source (`chooseFormat`'s `losslessOnly`: it halves instead of going lossy) and lossy from a JPEG. It goes through `encodeImageFile`'s options argument, which is absent on every drop, so drops are unchanged (pinned to goldens). The pre-snap originals are stashed under the existing rule only (a snap fired), so a 16-material import (up to ~48 textures) can still churn the 32-record origin LRU and evict an earlier "Revert to original" record — accepted, the cache being a device-local convenience.
- **What the conversion does (WebP + power of two)**: a dropped image is re-encoded to **WebP as far as the running browser will go** — lossless WebP for sources that must stay exact (PNG, and `.webp` re-drops whose container says VP8L), lossy WebP q0.85 for photographic sources, with a quality ladder (0.85→0.75→0.65) tried before any dimension is thrown away. Support AND losslessness are **probed at runtime** on an 8×8 gradient (`probeEncodeCaps`), never inferred: WebKit silently returns PNG from a WebP request (still true in 2026, and it covers the Tauri WKWebView build, not just Safari), and the HTML spec leaves `quality` UA-defined for WebP, so Chromium's "q=1 means lossless" is an implementation detail. `chooseFormat` is a FALL-THROUGH, not a preference — making the lossless branch absolute would hand every over-budget PNG to the halving retry and land a 2048² screenshot at 256-512px. **Alpha never yields JPEG** on any capability combination (pinned by test). Dimensions are then snapped to a power of two **ALWAYS**, under "convert" (`imageCodec.ts`, owner rule 2026-09-10: "use power of two always; jump up if it is near 80 percent"): per axis INDEPENDENTLY, an axis at or above `POT_ROUND_UP_RATIO` (0.8) of the next power of two rounds UP to it (1920 → 2048, 1700 → 2048) unless that exceeds the device cap, anything else rounds DOWN (1080 → 1024, 1280 → 1024, 1600 → 1024) — so 1920×1080 lands on 2048×1024 (+1 % pixels), never on a square, the largest growth on an axis is 1.25× and the largest shrink just under 1.6×. A rounded-UP target that blows the payload budget falls back to the rounded-DOWN one (`potFloorTarget`, never larger than the base that already fit). **The snap never trades losslessness for a round-up**: when the base encode is lossless, the power-of-two pass tries only the lossless candidates, so a lossless PNG whose round-up does not fit losslessly lands on the floor target instead of silently going lossy — and when the floor does not fit losslessly either, the NPOT base ships. That is not rare: a dithered or pixel-pattern PNG resamples into anti-aliased pixels that no longer compress (measured: a dithered 1700² PNG, 288 K lossless at its own size, fits the 1024 floor only lossy). It REPLACED a conservative snap — nearest POT only within 1.25× up / 1.15× down (1280×720 stayed NPOT), skipped entirely for binary-alpha cutouts and low-colour sources — and those skips are GONE with "always": pixel art, UI sprites, masks and hard cutouts are now resampled too, which blurs hard pixels and frays a cutout's edge against `alphaTest`. The escape hatches are what keep that acceptable: "Revert to original" restores the exact pre-snap encode, and answering **No** in the import dialog still stores an image untouched (no format change, no snap) — the right answer for a normal map or pixel art. `PixelStats` is `{ alpha }` now; the colour count and binary-alpha flag existed only for the skips. **nothing in this stack needs POT** — three r184 has no `makePowerOfTwo`/`textureNeedsPowerOfTwo` path left (that was WebGL1/ES2) and both WebGL2 and WebGPU sample NPOT with RepeatWrapping and full mipmaps — so it is a deliberate hygiene choice rather than a correctness fix, and the resample goes through a **wrapped border** (`POT_WRAP_MARGIN`): plain `drawImage` clamps at the edges, so a seamless tile would come back with a seam on every boundary, in a node whose Repeat setting defaults ON. The pre-snap encode is ALWAYS produced first and kept as the node's "original", and the snap is declined outright when that stash can't be made — a destructive step never ships without its escape hatch. Conversion is automatic at drop with an in-flight ghost at the drop point (transform/opacity only, so the compositor keeps it animating while the encoder blocks) and multi-file drops are **serialized** (N concurrent encoders compounded into one freeze). Honest progress is impossible — `toBlob`/`toDataURL` are single opaque calls with no callback and no cancel.
- **The Image node's RESOLUTION is a choice** (`resolutionLadder` in imageCodec.ts + `resizeEncodedImage` in imageImport.ts, 2026-09-09): the settings menu's Resolution row is a dropdown offering POWER-OF-TWO sizes derived from the ORIGINAL — the one lever that turns "this shader is too expensive" into something actionable, since a 4 K photo on a blurred backdrop costs the same bandwidth as one that matters. Six rules. It re-encodes from the ORIGINAL (`imageOriginCache`), never from what is stored — 2048 → 1024 → 512 through the current payload would stack three lossy passes and could never go back UP — which is also why the ladder is ANCHORED to the original's dimensions rather than to the current ones: an anchor that moved with each pick could only ever descend. **Every rung is a POWER OF TWO** (2026-09-10): the top rung is the original snapped by the drop's own 80 % rule and capped at the device (`potTarget`), each further rung halves both axes — so a freshly converted image sits ON the top rung, and a 1920×1080 photo's ladder reads 2048×1024 / 1024×512 / 512×256 / 256×128. A rung whose size IS the source's (a source that was already POT within the cap) is marked `original` and routed through `revert()` rather than re-encoded, since re-encoding would spend a lossy pass to reach a worse copy of a file already in hand; any other source's exact size is not a rung, and Revert is the way back to it. Rungs are keyed `${w}x${h}`, never by divisor — with a POT anchor "divisor 1" means the snapped original, which is usually NOT the original, and `imageResolution.test.ts` fails on `divisor === 1` reappearing. An NPOT payload — stored before the snap became unconditional, or declined at drop — sits on no rung, so its size is shown as its own `current` option rather than leaving the box reading someone else's number. The floor is a SHORT-side rule (`RESOLUTION_MIN_DIM` **8**, owner 2026-09-10: "allow to go as low as 8px" — it was 64 behind a fixed four-rung list; the ladder now halves until the short side would drop below 8, so a 2048×1024 original offers eight rungs down to 16×8, and every rung at or below 256 px pays the cost floor, ×0.5) so a panorama is not cut off early, and no rung exceeds the device cap: the anchor lands ON the cap (a POT-floored one — `clampPotCap`) and halvings only shrink. **A large step down goes through a 2:1 PYRAMID first** (`resizeEncodedImage`): one `drawImage` from 2048 to 8 is a bilinear sample of a few dozen of four million pixels, i.e. aliased noise rather than an average of the picture; an exact halving averages each 2×2 block and never samples past the edge (every destination pixel maps between four source texels), so it is seam-safe for a tile and leaves the wrapped resample only the final step of less than 2×. MEASURED on a period-3 stripe pattern (true mean red 170): single-step pixels spanned 128–255 in Chrome 152, WebKit 26.5 and Firefox 153, the pyramid 169–171 in all three — and a 1px red/blue pattern is the WRONG probe for this (at a power-of-two ratio every bilinear tap lands exactly between two columns and averages by accident, which is how the first check reported single-step as fine). `resizeEncodedImage` deliberately repeats `encodeImageFile`'s TAIL rather than calling it: that function's job is the DROP decision (source-pixel guard, EXIF orientation, device cap, POT snap, and the halving retry — which here would hand back a resolution other than the one picked), while what is genuinely shared is the wrapped resample (Repeat defaults ON, so a plain resample seams every tile), `chooseFormat`, the quality ladder and the payload budget. And this path may AWAIT where Revert and the Data-map flip may not: those await a READ and then write TWO things, which is what would split them into two undo entries, whereas here the whole await precedes a SINGLE `updateNodeData` — the node is re-read after it (the menu can outlive its node), the project-wide budget is re-checked only when the new encode GROWS the payload — a pick that shrinks it is never refused, even in a project already over budget (it used to be, with the Revert notice's wording) — and a refusal raises its OWN `image-resolution-cap` notice (N6) rather than borrowing `image-revert-cap`. The refusal is on BYTES, and a step DOWN can grow the bytes (a lossless source that fits a lower rung only lossy comes back longer), so the notice says "Raising" only when the pick adds pixels (`resize.raising`) and "Resizing" otherwise; like every image notice it names the file by its STORED extension (`displayImageFileName`), as the card does, a failed encode leaves the node untouched, and `srcWidth`/`srcHeight` are set to the ORIGINAL's dimensions, which is what those keys have always meant. **The ladder's SOURCE is the stashed original when there is one, else the payload ITSELF while it has never been resized** (`ladderSource`, 2026-09-10) — the origin cache is written only by a drop-time snap or by a resize, so for the ordinary unsnapped drop there is no record and none is needed until the first pick, which STASHES the exact payload it is about to replace (`stashImageOrigin` inside `applyResolution`, `originId` + `srcWidth`/`srcHeight` written in the same `updateNodeData`). That is what puts the control on every image node, with no IndexedDB round trip to appear. Stashing on EVERY drop was tried first (2026-09-09) and reverted the same day, on an audit: every drop then competed for the cache's 32 slots, so an ordinary drop could evict the ONE record that undoes a destructive snap (`potTarget` is conservative, so snaps are a minority of drops), every drop paid a `getAll` + sort, and the study clean slate became responsible for bytes it never had to hold. `canKeepOriginal` gates that first pick — `canStashPayload`, the same rule `payloadToRecord` applies, asked up front: a payload the cache would refuse (>600 K, i.e. placed under ignore-limits) is never resized, or the resize ships with no way back, and the read-only row names THAT reason rather than blaming device-local storage. The `original` rung on a payload-sourced ladder is a no-op (it already is the original); on a stashed one it is `revert()`. **The Original row + Revert button LATCH for the menu session** (`receiptRef`, keyed on `nodeId|originId` — the node AND its payload lineage: a second right-click MOVES the menu without remounting it, and a later lineage change on the same node, such as a texture pick, must not keep a stale "not on this device" row up): their gate is "the payload differs from the original", which is false on the very frame a revert lands, so without the latch the block unmounted under the cursor and the documented receipt — the button dropping to "already the original" — could never render. A revert keeps `originId`, so the receipt survives it. **And the original the menu READ belongs to ONE `originId`** (`menus/imageOriginView.ts`, pure + tested, 2026-09-12): the cache read is async and the menu's state outlives the node it was read for, so it is held as `{ id, payload }` and `origin`/`pending` are DERIVED from it — no original and `pending` until the read for the id the node carries NOW answers. Before that the menu kept "the last payload read": moved from node A to node B, it showed A's original with Revert LIVE, and a click wrote A's picture into B (the ladder would have re-encoded from A's original too); and an id going to '' mid-read left `pending` stuck at true, which disabled B's Resolution select for the rest of the menu session. A menu moved onto a CLONE reuses the read — correct, the cache is content-keyed — while the receipt, per node, does not carry. `imageOriginView.test.ts` runs the moved-menu sequence through a model of the component (tied to it by source pins) AND through a transcription of the old menu, so each scenario is shown to fail the old code rather than pass vacuously. `resized` (was `wasSnapped`) is the pair `srcWidth`/`srcHeight`, written by the snap AND by a resize, deleted by a revert; it is the truthful name now that a resolution pick is the common way those keys appear on a never-snapped node. The control still disables itself with the device-local explanation when a RESIZED image's original has left this device (shared project, another browser, aged out of the LRU). **The loop is closed**: `imageNodeCost` prices the node by its stored resolution (see the `complexity.json` entry above), so a rung down is a visibly cheaper badge.
- **"Revert to original" (Image node settings)**: restores the ORIGINAL payload — the pre-snap encode for a drop the power-of-two snap fired on, or the pre-resize payload once a Resolution was picked (the cache is written at exactly those two moments and no other; `clearImageOrigins()` empties it for the study clean slate, since a participant's dropped source images must not stay recoverable from the study machine and the consent text covers neither cache). For the snap that means the payload the import WOULD have produced without the POT snap — same EXIF strip, same device texture cap, same 600 K budget; NEVER the raw source file (that would re-embed the GPS the canvas round-trip exists to strip, and blow every cap). Bytes live in `utils/imageOriginCache.ts` (IndexedDB, `previewMeshCache`'s throw-safe shape), **not** on `values`: `totalImageChars`/`sanitizeImageNodes` inspect only `imageB64`, so a second payload key would be invisible to the 600 K per-image cap, the 3 M project cap, the 8 M hard ceiling and the whitelist — a tampered `.fastshader` could smuggle an unbounded blob past the trust boundary — and it would ride ~101 history clones, the autosave, the clipboard and the project embed. Only `originId` (+ `srcWidth`/`srcHeight`) ride the node, all three whitelisted in `sanitizeImageNodes`. The record is **content-keyed** (a digest of the stashed payload): it survives Ctrl+D/paste, which mint fresh node ids while cloning `data`, and a re-drop under a different headset texture cap becomes a DIFFERENT record instead of silently rewriting what an existing node reverts to. Reads are re-validated as adversarial (`recordToPayload`: version, key-matches-digest, whitelist, dimensions). Device-local by design, so the row disables with an explanatory tooltip after sharing a project, in another browser, or once it ages out (32 records / 24 MB LRU on the web; 64 records / 256M chars on desktop, `utils/platformCaps.ts`, where each record may reach the 6M per-image cap so the character bound binds first, and the WKWebView/WebView2 IndexedDB quota at that size is unverified). Ticking **"Data map (linear, no mipmaps)" also restores the original** — that checkbox is the only moment the app learns an image is a normal/height map (the drop cannot know), and a resampled normal map has wrong normals; both changes ride ONE `updateNodeData` so it stays a single undo entry, which is why the record is prefetched when the menu opens, and re-read whenever the node's `originId` changes (`ImageNodeSettings` is its own component precisely because it needs that hook — an awaited read inside the handler would split one click into two undo steps).
- **Image node**: drag-dropped images become an `imageNode` (mirrors the Data node pipeline; since GLB Phase 4 the node can also be added EMPTY from the palette/Add-node menu, and since 2026-09-18 it is OFFERED there — see the Texture-node bullet; the file-drop path is not an add surface and is never gated). Payload = whitelisted `data:` URL on `values.imageB64` — **adversarial**: codegen re-encodes the decoded bytes (`decodeImageNode` → `bytesToBase64`, never the stored string), the ShaderNode thumbnail renders only `validImageDataUrl()`, malformed payloads degrade to an inert `vec3(0,0,0)`. **The payload does NOT sit in the editor's code**: graphToCode emits `img.src = "fs-asset:<node>-<hash>"; // name, WxH, size` and `utils`-style pure helpers in `engine/imageAssets.ts` splice the real `data:` URL back in at the surfaces that run or export the module as a standalone document (XR popup, A-Frame tab, Download Shader) — the sandboxed preview instead receives each payload ONCE per document and resolves the placeholders in the iframe (`engine/previewAssetFeed.ts`, see the preview-rebuild convention) — a word-wrapped 600K-char literal otherwise buried the shader under ~8,500 rows in Monaco and re-tokenized megabytes per graph edit. Safe because image nodes are already one-way through codeToGraph, so nothing ever read that payload back; the placeholder carries a payload hash so changing an image's BYTES still changes `code` and invalidates the code-keyed preview memos. An unknown placeholder key is left verbatim and degrades via the existing decode() fallback. Emission = FLAT module-scope statements with top-level await (never an async IIFE — codeToGraph's ReturnStatement visitor would hijack the output): `new Image()` → `try { await decode() } catch` → 1×1 fallback → `THREE.Texture` with RepeatWrapping, `flipY` written explicitly from the texture spec (true; false under the glTF orientation), sRGB or linear data-map per `values.colorSpace` ('color' | 'data'); the Fn body samples `texture(tex, uvExpr).rgb` (vec3 out; `uv` input falls back to `uv()`). **Channel sockets (the Texture node, GLB Phase 4).** Besides `out` (Color, vec3, still `outputs[0]`), the node offers Alpha, R, G and B (float, always mounted; ids `alpha`/`r`/`g`/`b`, ONE Map-only table in `utils/imageChannels.ts`). The registry declares them after `out` in the table's order (pinned in `imageChannelSockets.test.ts` and `imageNodeAddable.test.ts`), rendered as labelled rows (see the labelled-rows bullet); they are `float`, so `portShapeForHandle` reports 1 and a channel wired into Color or Environment is widened to `vec3(imageN.a)` like any scalar. All four are SWIZZLES OF ONE SAMPLE, the toHsl shape. While no channel socket is wired the emission is byte-identical — `texture(…).rgb`, consumers read the bare var (`registry/imageOutOnlyByteStability.test.ts`, tampered handles included). Wiring any of them (`imageSampleIsWide` in graphToCode, the ONE predicate the emitter and `resolveEdgeRef` share) emits `texture(…).rgba` once, and `resolveEdgeRef` hands out `.rgb` for `out` (also for a null or tampered handle) and `.a`/`.r`/`.g`/`.b` for the channels; the invalid-payload fallback is then `vec4(0, 0, 0, 1)`, because a vec3 cannot carry `.a`. The wide sample MUST stay the member form `.rgba`, never a bare `texture(...)` declarator: codeToGraph ignores a call-object member with a warning, which is what keeps an Apply inert, whereas a bare call becomes an `unknown` node that re-emits `_imageN_tex` after the Apply dropped its declaration — a ReferenceError that kills the module. No codeToGraph inverse map, because image nodes stay one-way. Points stay per node: one sample however many sockets are wired (`engine/imageChannelSockets.test.ts`, which also builds the chain against the real `three/tsl` — every swizzle reaches the SAME TextureNode). **Image nodes SHARE their module-scope objects** (`engine/imageTexturePlan.ts`, the ONE planner): one `THREE.Texture` per payload × `readImageTextureSpec` (colour space, filter, wrap, flipY — everything set on the Texture OBJECT), so two nodes holding the same image upload once; the `new Image()` + `decode()` is keyed by the payload ALONE, so they decode once and inline one `data:` URL even when their texture settings differ (a colour and a data map of one picture: one element, two Textures over it — pixel-neutral, since the element is only the upload's source). Payloads are grouped on the full re-encoded `src`, never on the FNV hash, which collides. Anything that differs only in the uv EXPRESSION samples the same texture: tile, offset, a wired uv or Direction, the Flip X/Y checkboxes (UV mirrors, NOT `Texture.flipY`, which is `true` unless the node carries the glTF orientation), and the glTF UV set, texture transform and normal-map green flip. The owner is the first node EMITTED, so it is declared before any sharer uses it, and only its `fs-asset:` placeholder appears in the code (placeholder keys stay per node, and `collectImageAssets` still maps every node); a graph without duplicate payloads emits byte-identically (`imageGraphByteStability.test.ts`). Every image-texture reference goes through the planner, Environment included — graphToCode constructs no `THREE.Texture` itself (`imageTexturePlan.test.ts` source-pins it). A new Texture-object setting must JOIN the spec, or two nodes differing in it silently share one texture. Points stay PER NODE: a second node is a genuine second sample. The planner's first-visit guard also closed a module-killer: an image node feeding two Raymarch per-step sockets is planned into both scopes and used to declare its setup twice (a duplicate `const`, measured as a SyntaxError). The Data node and the Stripes, Data Viz and Colormap bakes still have that shape (follow-up). **Normal-map decode**: an image's Color (`out`) socket wired into the Output node's `normal` socket is a tangent-space normal MAP, not a raw normal — graphToCode wraps that channel in TSL's `normalMap(...)` (which does the `*2-1` remap + TBN tangent→view transform + normalize) rather than feeding the raw [0,1] sample into `normalNode`. `normalMap()` needs LINEAR input, so `NodeEditor.onConnect` auto-switches the image to the `data` colorSpace on connect (skipped if the same image also drives an sRGB channel — one texture, one colorSpace; mutated via `setNodes` so connect+flip is a single undo step). A channel socket (Alpha/R/G/B) is a scalar: it is never decoded and never flips the colour space (`isImageChannelHandle` gates both graphToCode and `applyConnection`). Non-image sources feeding `normal` stay a raw normal override (unwrapped). NodeSettingsMenu has a "UV / Texture" section (values read Number-coerced by codegen): `tileX/tileY`, `offsetX/offsetY`, `repeat` (off → ClampToEdge), `flipX`/`flipY`, and the data-map toggle. All five inputs (`uv`, `tileX/tileY`, `offsetX/offsetY`) follow the SAME opt-in `exposedPorts` rules as the noise nodes (see NODE_DESIGN_REQUIREMENTS.md → "Exposed parameter sockets"): hidden until checked in the generic NodeSettingsMenu sections, auto-exposed when an edge arrives (useSyncEngine + loadGraph migration), wired edge overrides the stored value, hiding drops the port's edges. Dragging a wire within snapping distance of a node with named input sockets forces its input name-tooltips visible (floated left of each socket — TypedHandle `reveal` prop; operator cards, the Output node, and collapsed groups opt out — generic/permanent labels); noise + Image nodes additionally reveal their hidden sockets as floating dots on the card's left edge — dimmed, layout untouched — and landing the connection exposes that port permanently (`connectionReveal.ts` + `RevealSockets.tsx` + onConnect/onReconnect auto-expose; the reveal radius IS the editor's snap radius, one shared `CONNECTION_RADIUS`). The Output node does NOT drag-reveal hidden channels (settings-menu only). On the card, imageNode input rows show ONLY the port label (`.shader-node__in-label`) — never inline number widgets (numbers are context-menu-only). When the graph embeds images, "Download Shader" produces a `.zip` (dependency-free STORE writer, `zipWriter.ts`): the self-contained `.js` + `images/<sanitized-name>.<real-mime-ext>`, ONE file per DISTINCT image (`collectImageFiles` compares MIME and decoded bytes in full; the first node's name wins; a skipped duplicate still advances the `image<n>` fallback counter; a graph without duplicates writes a byte-identical zip) + README. Import accepts `.js` AND `.zip` on every surface — Load Script, the code-panel drop, and the canvas drop — via the shared `projectImport.ts` path (`zipReader.ts` picks the `.js` carrying the FASTSHADERS_PROJECT_V1 block; the loose image files are ignored on import since the payloads ride in the `.js`). **An exported image still rides twice by design: once in the module (which must run standalone; one literal per distinct payload since the texture sharing) and once PER NODE in the project block.** FastShaders 0.3.33 and older read pixels ONLY from `values.imageB64`. An ADDITIVE `imageRefs` field keeps a block parseable, not its pixels: such a file opens there with the images black and no notice, and a re-save from that build destroys them for good. So `engine/projectImageRefs.ts` ships the READER (`applyProjectToStore` recovers a top-level ref from the module's own `data:` literals before P2a's in-node refs resolve, both passes on ONE `RefBudget`; a ref the module cannot satisfy raises the `images-missing` notice, and each node left without pixels is reported once), while the WRITER stays behind `EXPORT_IMAGE_REFS = false`, the owner's decision. images/ and the module are free to de-duplicate because pixel RESTORE reads the per-node block copies. The module stays self-contained and still carries every distinct payload once, which is all Podest/A-Frame and the top-level imageRefs reader need. NB the raw sample renders mirrored left-right in the preview, so codegen bakes the `1-u` correction into the DEFAULT; the Flip X checkbox (unchecked by default) mirrors relative to the corrected look — checking it emits the raw `uv()`. **The CARD SHOWS the flips** (2026-09-09, owner request): the thumbnail is mirrored per axis (`transform: scale(±1, ±1)`) whenever `flipX`/`flipY` is ticked — a checkbox in a menu you must close before you can see its effect is a guess. The sign convention is the trap and the two sides must agree: `graphToCode` reads `mirrorX = flipX < 0.5` (the UNCHECKED default is what carries the 1-u correction) and `mirrorY = flipY >= 0.5`, so on the card each ticked box mirrors what is drawn there, and both sides test at the same `>= 0.5` threshold (`imageThumbFlip.test.ts`). Under the glTF orientation the texture is uploaded unflipped and there is no baked correction, so `mirrorX` becomes `flipX >= 0.5` as well: each ticked box still mirrors, on the card and in the shader alike. `transform` is safe on that `<img>` — the card's hover lift is a `scale:` on `.node-base`, a different element. **Filtering is a choice** (`values.filter`, 2026-09-10, owner request: "linear or closest"): Linear (three's default) or **Nearest** — the API's own term (three `NearestFilter`, WebGL `GL_NEAREST`, WebGPU `'nearest'`), "nearest-neighbour" in prose. Stored as the string `graphToCode` compares against with an EXACT `=== 'nearest'`; absent, `'linear'` or junk is linear and emits NOTHING new, so every image saved before the option is byte-identical (`engine/imageNode.test.ts` sweeps junk values against both colour spaces). Nearest sets `magFilter = NearestFilter` and, on a colour image (which keeps its mipmaps), `minFilter = NearestMipmapNearestFilter` — crisp at every distance without the shimmer plain nearest minification gives; on a data map (no mipmaps) both are `NearestFilter`. The card shows it — `image-rendering: pixelated` on the thumbnail, or the browser smooths an 8 px rung into a blur the shader never draws (`imageThumbFlip.test.ts` pins that card and codegen read the same string). Like the flips and the colour space it is not in `PRESERVED_KEYS`, so a Reset returns it to Linear. **The glTF mapping is four settings over eight keys** (`utils/imageUvMapping.ts`, GLB Phase 4 Step 7; written by the Phase 5 importer through `gltfTextureValues`, and by hand in the settings menu's collapsed **glTF mapping** block, `menus/ImageMappingSettings.tsx`, which is hidden in study sessions — stored keys still emit there, because the gate is UI-only). Each is read EXACTLY by `readImageUvMapping`, the one reader, so an absent, junk or default value emits today's module byte for byte (`registry/imageOutOnlyByteStability.test.ts` sweeps junk under every key; valid values are the only thing that moves bytes). `orientation: 'gltf'` sets `Texture.flipY = false` and drops the baked 1-u correction, so a ticked Flip box mirrors on both axes; it is the ONLY key in the texture-spec share key, so two nodes with one payload and different orientations get two textures over one element, and everything else shares. `uvSet: 1|2|3` (numbers only — `Number(true)` is 1, and `'1'` is never written) samples `uv(n)`, three's `uv1`..`uv3` = TEXCOORD_1..3, the digit coming from the reader's closed table; a mesh without that set samples texel (0,0) as one flat colour with only a console warning (AttributeNode), which the row's title says, and a wired uv input wins over it. The five `xf*` keys are KHR_texture_transform (finite, |v| ≤ 1e6), applied FIRST — base (wired uv ?? `uv(n)` ?? `uv()`) → transform → mirror → tile → offset, and a wired Direction still replaces all of it — as ONE `mat2(m00, m01, m10, m11).mul(base)` of JS-precomputed constants (ROW-major: with all-number arguments TSL's `mat2` builds a `THREE.Matrix2`, whose constructor takes rows — the column-major order three's RotateNode uses applies only to NODE arguments, which take the JoinNode path, and emitting it here rendered the transpose, i.e. every rotation turned the wrong way, until `imageUvTransformTsl.test.ts` started running the emitted text through real three; `.mul(vec2(sx, sy))` when there is no rotation) plus an offset, snapped at 1e-12 so a quarter turn emits clean `0`/`1`/`-1` and never −0. It follows the Khronos REFERENCE renderer — scale, then rotation about the UV origin, then offset — which three's GLTFLoader (`Matrix3.setUvTransform`, scaling AFTER rotating) matches only for UNIFORM scale, so a parity check against GLTFLoader needs uniform-scale fixtures (`imageUvMapping.test.ts` pins both halves). `normalGreen: 'flip'` turns the Output's decode into `normalMap(ref, vec2(1, -1))`, GLTFLoader's `normalScale.y *= -1` for a primitive without TANGENT, inside the channel-handle gate, so a scalar socket is never decoded, flipped or not. vec2 is imported exactly when a step writes one (a rotation-only transform imports only `mat2`). The writers delete a key at its default, so a node toggled on and off is JSON-identical to one never touched; `sanitizeImageNodes` drops malformed keys on all three restore paths (a resource bound — emission reads strictly anyway); and a Reset keeps all eight (`PRESERVED_KEYS`). The card thumbnail shows the picture as stored, never its mapping onto a mesh, so on a built-in shape a glTF-oriented texture renders turned 180° while the card stays upright — the orientation row's title says so. `engine/imageUvTransformTsl.test.ts` builds the emitted `mat2`/`uv(n)`/`normalMap(x, vec2)` against the real `three/tsl`. **Any image the project already holds can be PICKED into a node, and a file can be loaded into one** (`menus/TexturePicker.tsx` + the pure `utils/textureSources.ts`, GLB Phase 4 Step 6; settings menu only, and hidden in study sessions by `isEvalMode()` — the node face, NodeVisual and the tiles are unchanged). The Image section's **Texture** row expands an inline grid of every DISTINCT valid payload in the project (`projectTextureSources`: keyed on the full string, never a hash; document order; at most `MAX_LISTED_TEXTURE_SOURCES` = 64 cells, the rest counted in a line), built through a cheap-key two-step selector (`textureSourcesKey`) and only while the grid is open. Choosing one is ONE synchronous `updateNodeData`, i.e. one undo entry, and a COPY, not a link: the payload moves with its provenance (`originId`, `srcWidth`/`srcHeight`, all from ONE holder, stale ones deleted — `withImagePayload`) while the node's own sampling settings stay, and because the original cache is content-keyed, Revert and the Resolution ladder keep working on a picked image. A pick that GROWS the payload is checked per INSTANCE (`imageCharsReplacing`, `PROJECT_IMAGE_BUDGET_COUNT`), so picking an image another node already holds is not free, and a refusal raises its own toggle-bearing `image-pick-cap` notice: `image-total-cap`'s "Add anyway" places a NEW node from a File, and a pick has neither. **"From file…"** runs the canvas drop's pipeline into THIS node (`fillImageNodeFromFile`, a module function because the menu can close or MOVE mid-encode): `encodeImageFile` with the remembered convert-or-keep answer — asked INLINE in the menu while the preference is 'ask', since a portalled dialog sits outside the menu and the press answering it would close the menu under it — then `resolveImageDrop`, the same per-instance check and `image-pick-cap`, and the drop's own announcements (a per-image refusal whose "Add anyway" refills this node through `proceed` instead of placing a new one, the device-downscale notice, and the import note's budget lines). The target node is re-read LIVE after the await and written once or not at all, and the busy state is keyed by node id, so a moved menu never shows another node as busy. This is what makes an EMPTY node (added from the palette, or stripped on restore) fillable. `TextureSource` is a discriminated union from day one (today only `'project'`), and both consumers — the pick handler and the grid's thumbnail — switch on `src.kind` with a `never` default, so Phase 5's `'model'` kind (materialised through the import pipeline, budget-checked like a drop, shown through a display URL distinct from its payload) cannot compile until each handles it. The grid is a nested scroller in ContextMenu.css under the menu's `nowheel` shell (`canvasWheel.test.ts`'s list is unchanged); `imagePicker.test.ts` and `textureSources.test.ts` pin it. **And `.shader-node__file-name` is node INK** (`rgba(var(--node-ink-rgb), 0.7)`), not the literal `#555555` it carried while a node body was always light: once `--node-bg` learned to flip, that grey on a near-black card MEASURED 1.82:1, i.e. the filename was invisible in dark mode. The alpha is nudged past the value that would reproduce `#555` exactly (0.65) because the dark side is what was reported — 8.2:1 light (was 6.7) and 6.6:1 dark. KNOWN, not fixed: the same "authored while nodes were light" class leaves `.shader-node__formula-chip`/`__mode-chip` (`#2d7dd2`) at 3.2:1 and the `--bad` chip / noise `±1` chip (`#e74c3c`) at 3.6:1 on a dark card — above the 3:1 UI threshold, below AA for their 8-9px size, and hue-carrying, so lifting them is a colour decision rather than a token swap. Context menus get enlarged type via `--font-size-*` token overrides scoped on `.context-menu` (ContextMenu.css) — all submenus + DragNumberInput inherit them. Limits (`imageNode.ts`, sized per build by `utils/platformCaps.ts`): 600K chars/image and 3M chars total on the web, 6M/32M on desktop, 64MP source guard — bypassable via the LimitModal checkbox (`fs:ignoreImageLimits`); the 8M-char hard ceiling always applies. **Every limit announces itself.** A drop that had to HALVE for the per-image budget, or store a lossless source lossy, says so in the canvas import note (`encodeImageFile`'s `budgetScaled`/`losslessDropped`, composed by the pure `utils/imageImportNote.ts`). There is one note per DROP, so a batch reports once with count-neutral lines — including an image the user places later with "Add anyway": every notice a drop raises carries its `dropId`, and `showImageDropReports` re-composes a still-visible note from the same drop instead of replacing it, whichever of the two finishes first. **The 3M total is counted on every path that ADDS a payload** (`exceedsImageBudget`: the PROJECT total per instance — `PROJECT_IMAGE_BUDGET_COUNT`, because every export still carries one copy per node and 0.3.33's import strips past 3M per instance; the LIBRARY total per distinct payload — `LIBRARY_IMAGE_BUDGET_COUNT` — since `fs:savedGroups` stores each once). The drop, paste, Ctrl+D, the right-click menu's Duplicate Node (`duplicateNodeWithinBudget` in menuShared.tsx) and saved-group instantiate raise `image-total-cap`. A path that REPLACES a node's payload instead — Revert, a Resolution pick, the texture picker and its "From file…" — is checked with `imageCharsReplacing`, only when the payload GROWS, and raises its own checkbox-override notice (`image-revert-cap`, `image-resolution-cap`, `image-pick-cap`). Saving a group raises `image-library-cap` against the library's OWN total (`MAX_LIBRARY_IMAGE_CHARS`): `fs:savedGroups` is a second localStorage document that re-materialises every payload on each write and shares the origin quota with `fs:graph`, and checking the group alone would be vacuous, since it is a subset of a project already under budget. A path with no File behind it overrides through `LimitNotice.proceed`, a callback `resolveLimitNotice` runs after committing the checkbox. That makes a notice non-serialisable, so `pendingLimitNotices` must never be persisted or cloned. A paste or group carrying no image payload is never refused, even in an over-budget project, and a refusal pushes no undo entry; a refused paste keeps the clipboard where it was. Loads (`fs:graph`, `fs:savedGroups`) keep hard caps only. Known gap: `placeTilePayload` logs `asset-drop` before `instantiateSavedGroup` can refuse, so a refused-then-cancelled group drop is still logged (moving it changes a reviewed telemetry chokepoint). The modal's `{name}` fallback is CASE-aware (`nameFor` in `limitNoticeCopy.ts`): nominative 'This image'/'Šis attēls', accusative 'this image'/'šo attēlu', genitive 'this image'/'šī attēla', plural accusative 'these images'/'šos attēlus'. One nominative fallback made Latvian ungrammatical in two slots and gave English a capital 'This' mid-sentence. English never goes through the disambiguated keys, because `t()` returns the key verbatim in English. localStorage quota failures (graph autosave + saved groups) surface a `storage-quota` LimitModal notice instead of failing silently. The notice names its slot through a typed `slot: 'graph' | 'savedGroups'` rather than a free-form `detail` string, because the English detail string is how the sentence reached Latvian users half in English. Images the HARD caps strip on the `fs:graph` / `fs:savedGroups` LOADS (`sanitizeImageNodes(…, false)`: a payload failing the whitelist or over 8M chars) are reported as `images-stripped-on-load`, with no Ignore-limits checkbox, since that checkbox cannot lift a hard ceiling. The report comes from App.tsx's mount effect, ABOVE its remembered-viewport early return, or a returning user never sees it. The saved-group library is written back when anything was stripped, so the notice fires once, exactly as the post-seed autosave already guarantees for `fs:graph`. **An Image node never samples KTX2** (owner decision, Phase 8, compatibility-first): an HTMLImageElement cannot decode a KTX2 container, and a CompressedTexture emission would be a third resolution mode that 0.6 and plain-three pages cannot run. So the shader's own textures stay WebP/PNG/JPEG, and KTX2 is only ever an extra `KHR_texture_basisu` source in the single-GLB export, for other viewers. `ktx2ImageNodeDecision.test.ts` pins it, and deleting that test is how the other option starts.
- **Selection**: **Shift+click adds to the selection** (`multiSelectionKeyCode={['Shift']}` — an array is "any of these", and it's module-scope because `useKeyPress` memoizes on its identity; Meta/Control LEFT that list on 2026-09-10, when ⌘/Ctrl+click became PREVIEW MODE — see the next bullet — since one modifier cannot both extend the selection and route a node to the 3D view). This REQUIRES `selectionKeyCode={null}`: Shift's React Flow default is hold-to-marquee, and while it's held the Pane's `onPointerDownCapture` stopPropagation()s a pointerdown that landed on a node and opens a rubber band instead — so Shift+click would marquee rather than extend. Nothing is lost, because `selectionOnDrag` already gives a modifier-free marquee from empty canvas (`isSelecting = selectionKeyPressed || userSelectionActive || selectionOnDrag`). Marquee stays mouse-only — `selectionOnDrag` is off on coarse pointers so the first finger of a two-finger pan can't start a selection box.
- **PREVIEW MODE — look at ONE node's output on the 3D view** (2026-09-10, owner request; `utils/nodePreview.ts` is the pure half, `components/NodeEditor/PreviewRoute.tsx` the line). Right-click a node → **Preview** (one row for a one-output node, one row per socket for `toHsl`/Split/a Data node's columns, "Stop preview" on the active one — in `menuShared`'s shared `NodeActions` footer, so every per-node menu offers it and the sinks, which have no output, get no row), or **⌘/Ctrl+click** the node (first output; the same click on the previewed node toggles it off, on another node switches). While it lasts the 3D preview renders THAT socket on the Output's Color with EVERY other input of the Output disabled, everything but the previewed node and the Output dims to 0.25 (nodes, wires, frames, notes — the symbolic Output→3D link is deliberately NOT dimmed, the Output still feeds the view), the previewed node takes the selected lift + border, and a STRAIGHT solid `--border-focus` line runs from its socket to the Output's Color socket (straight so it can never be mistaken for a wire that exists). It ends on a press anywhere else, on Escape, or when the graph is replaced (import / NEW). Six rules. (1) **Nothing is an edit.** The state is `nodePreview` in the store, session-only like `hoveredNodeId` — never persisted, never in history, never in a shared file — and the reroute happens in a DERIVED graph only the sync engine's `previewCode` pass sees (`previewGraph`: unwrap, drop every edge into any sink, replace the target Output — `findDefaultOutput`, deliberately NOT `defaultOutput`, since the point of the rewrite is to STRIP that node's binding and materials and a TARGETED one has to be reached too — with a CLEAN one at the same id carrying only its `materialSettings` and the active flag (so that node's mesh targets, `gltfMaterialIndex`, `modelSignature` and `modelMeshes` are all dropped ON PURPOSE: the previewed socket is shown on everything that node shades), strip every other sink's flag so a Raymarch Output cannot outrank it, synthesize an Output under `PREVIEW_OUTPUT_ID` when the graph has none, add ONE edge socket→`color`); `graphToCode` needs no preview branch, and codegen's own Color rules widen a scalar/vec2 and coerce a logic node exactly as a hand-wired socket would. **KNOWN LIMIT since the per-material Output split, not fixed**: `previewGraph` replaces exactly ONE node, so on a document with several Output nodes — which every GLB import produces — the other TARGETED ones still contribute their own `parts` / `materialParts` entries and go on painting their meshes. Preview mode is whole-model only on a single-Output document; elsewhere the previewed socket lands on whatever the replaced node shades, and nothing says so on screen. `code`, the export and the panel keep the real graph: `setCode(code, 'graph', previewCode?)` grew the third argument so the two land in ONE write, and the graph→code effect threads `previewChanged` through both of its guards the way `revalidated` is. (2) **A code→graph pass ENDS it** (`doCodeSync`'s first line): `requestCodeSync` has already put the panel's text on the preview and with `syncSource` at 'code' nothing emits the reroute, so a mode left set would read as on while showing the applied code, then snap back on the first scrub. The sync engine also clears a target whose node or socket is gone (`resolveNodePreview`), and NodeEditor clears on `fs:graph-imported`/`fs:graph-new` because a re-import keeps the same ids. (3) **The outside press is CAPTURE-phase `pointerdown`** (ContextMenu's closer reasoning: React Flow stops click propagation, and a press that becomes a drag never clicks), exempting the node itself (scrubbing its own number boxes is part of looking at it), `PREVIEW_KEEP_SELECTOR` — the menus a right-click on it opens with their portalled popovers, and the WHOLE 3D pane, since orbiting the model is looking at the preview — and the middle button, since panning is not a click. A press on another node ends this preview BEFORE that node's own click/row starts its own. (4) **The two node marks are ATTRIBUTES (`data-fs-preview="src"|"dst"`), not classes — measured, not taste.** React Flow's wrapper renders its `className` from `selected`/`dragging`, React writes the WHOLE string when that prop changes, and a hand-added class goes with it: ⌘/Ctrl+click both selects and previews, and the `selected` write landed AFTER the effect's `classList.add` (MutationObserver, old value carrying the class, new value without) — the previewed node dimmed with the rest. `fs-menu-active` and `fs-labels-shown` are classes and carry the same latent weakness; they survive only because a menu closes on any press. (5) **The line is DOM-tracked per rAF** like PreviewLink — the source's `.react-flow__handle.source[data-handleid]`, the Output's Color input, each falling back to the card's edge midpoint when unmounted — but sits ABOVE the node layer (z 5, mounted before the panels so the canvas bar still wins), and is not drawn at all when the graph has no plain Output on the canvas (the module then feeds the synthesized one). (6) **PROBE TRAPS**: the preview iframe's `srcdoc` does NOT change on a shader edit — the hot-swap path posts the module as an `fs:shader` message, so verify by listening inside the frame, not by grepping `srcdoc`; and under `vite dev` the live store module is `useAppStore.ts?t=<hmr>`, so `import('/FastShaders/src/store/useAppStore.ts')` from the page yields a SECOND, empty instance — take the URL from `performance.getEntriesByType('resource')`. MEASURED in Chrome: menu and ⌘+click both enter, the iframe receives `return { colorNode: vec3(sub1) }` and gets the real module back on Escape, the code panel never changes, the undo button stays disabled, and a click in the 3D pane keeps the mode. Not logged to eval telemetry (display-only; adding it means a new chokepoint in `evalHooks.test.ts`'s reviewed list). `nodePreview.test.ts` pins the derived graph against the real codegen and source-pins the wiring.
- **Groups**: selection groups are first-class React Flow nodes (`type: 'group'`) created via Ctrl+G or right-click → Group Selection. **A new group takes a random colour from the DIMMED colour-node palette** (`GROUP_COLOR_PALETTE` = `NEW_COLOR_PALETTE.map(c => dimColor(c, 0.45))`, 2026-09-09, owner request) rather than the one indigo every group used to arrive in — a graph of frames otherwise read as one repeated colour until each was opened and recoloured by hand. Three rules. It is DERIVED from the colour-node palette, never a second hand-picked table: "the same palette, dimmer" is the whole rule, so `newNodeValues.test.ts` pins the RELATIONSHIP (one entry per hue, lower chroma, same channel order — mixing toward a NEUTRAL is what keeps the hue) instead of the hexes. It is dimmed IN WHAT IS STORED, not in the renderer: `getGroupFrameColors` already mixes 0.18 into the node body for the fill, but the SELECTED border is the stored colour at full strength, and dimming there would restyle every group saved before this. And it follows the colour node's spread rule — prefer a hue no group on the canvas is wearing (`pickSpreadColor`, shared by both, compared lower-cased), because with five entries a uniform roll repeats about one time in five, which is the sameness it exists to fix. **Only `groupSelection` rolls one**: `instantiateSavedGroup` restores the colour the group was saved with, `codeGroupBuilder` carries each built-in's own, and a group with no colour at all still falls back to `GROUP_DEFAULT_COLOR`, so every existing group keeps its indigo. **A new group is also NUMBERED** — `Group 1`, `Group 2`, … (`nextGroupLabel`, same day and the same reason: a canvas of frames all called "Group" names nothing). `max + 1` over the groups already matching `Group <n>` exactly, the `nextPropertyName` convention — the number is a label, not an identity, so a gap left by a deleted group is not reused and a RENAMED group ("Fresnel") neither counts nor blocks. Not retroactive: a group already on the canvas keeps its bare "Group", since renaming it would be an edit nobody asked for, and `GroupNode` still falls back to "Group" for an empty label. They have no registry entry and no shader semantics — `graphToCode`/`cpuEvaluator` ignore the *node*, but call `unwrapCollapsedGroupEdges()` from `edgeUtils.ts` at their entry to translate visually-rewritten boundary edges back to their real child endpoints, so collapse state never affects compiled output. Groups can be recolored, renamed, collapsed (members hidden via `display: none` className — *not* React Flow's `hidden: true`, which would unmount rAF loops; the toggle anchors at the frame's TOP-RIGHT corner — the pill appears at the old top-right and expanding grows leftward from the pill's top-right, `groupCollapse.test.ts` pins the round-trip). **A group frame's size has ONE home: top-level `width`/`height` plus the `data.width`/`data.height` mirror** — and `groupFrameSize` is the shared reader, because there used to be two. `groupSelection` wrote the canonical pair while `codeGroupBuilder` (EVERY built-in preset and texture) wrote `style: { width, height }` and nothing else. React Flow renders both identically — `node.width ?? node.style?.width` — so the divergence was invisible until something ASKED the node how big it was, and `toggleGroupCollapsed` asks: it read `node.width ?? data.width ?? 200`, found neither, and recorded 200x120 as the remembered expanded size. MEASURED in Chromium: a dropped preset rendered 300x428, collapsed to the pill, and **expanded to a 200x120 stub with its whole graph outside the frame** — unrecoverable by mouse, since the corner grip only exists on an expanded frame and React Flow's resizer refuses to shrink a parent below its children. The builder emits the canonical shape now; `groupFrameSize` still reads `style` (and `measured`) so every group already saved that way is repaired without a migration; and the expand DROPS `style`, so a group is normalized to one shape the first time it is touched. Two further guards on the expand, both because falling back to `currentWidth` there means falling back to THE PILL: a remembered dimension is honoured only if it is a finite number ≥ the resize grip's own minimum (`MIN_GROUP_W`/`MIN_GROUP_H`, shared with `GroupNode`'s `NodeResizeControl` so the two cannot drift), otherwise the frame is rebuilt around its members; and a remembered size is floored at "the frame contains its members" — UNPADDED, because a member flush against the edge is a real layout and a padded floor would grow every tight frame by 24px per expand, and skipped entirely when any member is UNMEASURED (right after a load, or in the node test env, where the 160x60 guess overestimates a real node enough to inflate a correct frame). A group ALREADY collapsed by the old code is repaired by an exact signature — a `style` size beside precisely `200x120`, which no other path can produce, since resizing first would have given the collapse a real `width` to read, saved to a per-browser library (`fs:savedGroups`), and dragged out of containers. Members never get `extent: 'parent'` — `onNodeDragStop` reconciles `parentId` after every drag instead. **Notes are ordinary members**: Ctrl+G sweeps a selected note in, dragging one into a frame adopts it, and it then rides the group's drags/collapse/ungroup/save-to-library like any node (a note is usually the LABEL for the cluster being grouped, so leaving it outside stranded the annotation). Notes stay exempt only from the placement rules that would fight their purpose — anti-overlap nudging (it would fling a note off the nodes it was deliberately dropped over) and drop-on-edge splicing — both guarded by `isNote` in NodeEditor's drag handlers, not by bailing out of membership. `addNote` PREPENDS, so ordering matters: a note is born parentless and both adoption paths re-sort it after its parent (`groupSelection` front-inserts the container; the drag handlers run the parent-before-children lift). `groupNotes.test.ts` pins all of it. `organizeSelection` still skips notes — auto-layout has no meaningful slot for a free-floating annotation. **A MARQUEE drawn inside a frame selects the frame only once it has selected EVERY member** (`components/NodeEditor/groupMarqueeSelection.ts`, 2026-09-09, owner request "in groups allow to do selections"). The canvas runs `SelectionMode.Partial`, so React Flow selects anything the band merely TOUCHES — right for a card, wrong for a frame, which is a big transparent backdrop that a band drawn anywhere inside it overlaps by construction: rubber-banding two nodes inside a group also grabbed the group, and dragging that moved the whole group with every member instead of the two nodes picked. Three things are load-bearing. The rule reads the MEMBERS, not the rectangle, and that is what makes it work at all: React Flow only re-proposes a selection when its own node SET changes (`areSetsEqual` in the Pane's `onPointerMove`), so a band grown to cover a frame without sweeping in a new node would never re-select it — deriving from members means the last member's own change carries the frame. It is gated on **`userSelectionRect`**, not `userSelectionActive`: the rect is set on the pane's pointerdown while the active flag is only set AFTER the first batch of select changes has been dispatched, so gating on the latter lets the opening sweep through. And it lives in NodeEditor's `onNodesChange` WRAPPER rather than the store's action, because the marquee lives in React Flow's store, which the app store cannot see — which is also what keeps `selectAll` (the A key, dispatching straight to the store action) taking frames along with everything else, as it is meant to. Two consequences are deliberate: an EMPTY frame is never marquee-selected (nothing to have taken; its header and the A key still select it), and a COLLAPSED pill is exempt entirely, since a pill stands in for a node and selects like one. **An expanded frame's only interactive surface is its HEADER** (drag, select, and the right-click GroupSettingsMenu): the body is `pointer-events: none` so the area inside a frame behaves like bare canvas — right-click opens the Add-node menu, drags rubber-band/pan, clicks don't select the group. This relies on React Flow already marking `.react-flow__viewport`/`.react-flow__nodes` pointer-events-none, so a pass-through frame leaves `.react-flow__pane` as the event target — which is what the Pane's `wrapHandler` guard requires before firing `onPaneContextMenu` (and it's why the touch long-press path, which dispatches a synthetic `contextmenu` on the pointerdown target, routes identically). Collapsed pills opt back in and stay fully interactive — a pill stands in for a node. The resize control opts back in too; see the pass-through block in `GroupNode.css`. **Groups resize exactly like notes**: a single `NodeResizeControl` at the BOTTOM-RIGHT with the notes' enlarged 15×15 grab box, rendered only while expanded AND selected — not React Flow's full `NodeResizer`, whose eight edge/corner handles ringed the frame and turned every near-miss along an edge into an accidental resize. So both surfaces now follow one rule: drag by the header, scale by the one corner. (Notes get that drag rule from `dragHandle: '.note-node__header'`; groups get it from the pointer-events pass-through above — different mechanism, same behaviour.)
- **Board drawings**: freehand ink annotations (DrawingLayer + DrawToolbar) are VISUAL-ONLY like notes/waypoints — invisible to graphToCode/cpuEvaluator/the sync engine. Strokes live in their own store slice, ride undo history BY REFERENCE (committed strokes are immutable; never structuredClone them), share the fs:graph autosave payload and the FASTSHADERS_PROJECT_V1 embed, and are sanitized as adversarial input on load/import (sanitizeDrawings caps). Same-opacity strokes must not compound where they cross: opacity is quantized and rendered via one SVG group-opacity isolation buffer per opacity level, and stroke colors are 6-digit hex ONLY (8-digit alpha would smuggle past the isolation).
- **An import auto-frames the canvas**: importing a shader replaces the nodes but NOT the viewport, so a project whose nodes sit anywhere but where the user is looking lands off-screen and reads as a failed import (an empty canvas). Every import surface funnels through `projectImport.ts`, which dispatches **`fs:graph-imported`** whenever the graph is actually replaced — deliberately NOT `fs:project-imported`, which also fires for a model-only zip where the graph never changed and re-framing would throw away the user's viewport. NodeEditor **ARMS** a fit on that event rather than fitting on it: a project block writes its nodes in the same tick the event fires (React hasn't rendered them), and a bare script has NO graph at all until `useSyncEngine`'s code→graph pass finishes a commit or two later — so the fit runs from the `[nodes]` effect, on the next graph the editor actually renders, behind the same double-rAF measurement guard `startNewShader` uses (`fitView` frames the MEASURED box; unmeasured nodes fit as zero-size points at maxZoom). Two details are load-bearing: the pending rAF is held in a REF torn down only on unmount, never by the effect's own cleanup — the cost pass writes the Output node's new total straight back into `nodes`, so an imported graph re-renders inside the two-frame window and a cleanup-owned rAF would be cancelled by exactly the change proving the import landed — and the arm carries a `IMPORT_FIT_TIMEOUT_MS` disarm, so an import that never yields a graph (parse errors, a byte-identical re-import that the sync engine skips) can't leave a fit primed to hijack a later unrelated edit. Desktop Work-folder loads get the FRAMING for free — same `importShaderText`/`importShaderZip` — but they do two things on top of the shared path (adopt the file's name, track it as the Save target), so "for free" is a claim about the fit only.
- **History**: circular buffer (50 entries) with undo/redo via `structuredClone`, Cmd+Z/Cmd+Shift+Z shortcuts, plus visible Undo/Redo buttons in the canvas's bottom-LEFT bar (`.fs-canvas-bar` — one pill also holding the Draw tools and the zoom −/+ / fit / canvas-bg controls that replaced the React Flow Controls stack; the buttons call `undo()`/`redo()` directly — equivalent to the keyboard path, since the graph→code effect re-runs on any nodes/edges identity change regardless). **Continuous gestures must be bracketed**: `beginInteraction()`/`endInteraction()` snapshot ONCE up front and suppress `pushHistory` until the gesture ends, so a DragNumberInput scrub is one undo entry instead of one per pointermove frame — and, just as importantly, the whole graph isn't `structuredClone`d 60×/s (megabytes once images are embedded). `beginInteraction` deliberately ignores `isUndoRedo` (that flag guards the sync engine's post-undo reconciliation; a pointer gesture is always a real mutation, and honouring a stale flag would silently swallow the gesture's history). DragNumberInput brackets on first move — not pointerdown, since a press that becomes a click-to-edit would push a no-op entry — and closes the bracket on pointerup, pointercancel, and unmount.
- **Preview rebuild is debounced**: until 2026-09-08 only property uniforms had a postMessage hot-update path and every other edit swapped `srcDoc`, re-fetching/re-parsing the ~1MB A-Frame bundle under a fresh opaque origin. **A module edit is now HOT-SWAPPED** (`fs:shader` → `SHADER_HOT_SWAP_SCRIPT` in tslToPreviewHTML, gen-tagged ack, a watchdog that escalates an unanswered swap to a real rebuild); `srcDoc` rebuilds only for the COLD keys ShaderPreview's `coldDocKey` comment lists. **Image payloads no longer ride each hot swap** (GLB Phase 6 S3, `engine/previewAssetFeed.ts`): the sandboxed preview receives `fs:image-assets` once per key per document — the boot module's keys baked as `window.__fsBootAssets`, later ones posted by `postShaderSwap` immediately BEFORE the `fs:shader` that needs them (postMessage is FIFO per target) — and `window.__fsResolveAssets` turns the `fs-asset:` placeholders into that document's `blob:` URLs right before a module Blob is minted, the boot blob and every swap alike. So a scrub on a graph holding megabytes of images posts the small placeholder module, not the payloads, and the module text the parent compares (`runningModuleRef`, the receiver's `applied`) stays placeholder-only. `sentAssetKeysRef` is the parent's per-document record of what the live document holds: seeded with the boot list on every rebuild AND again on the fresh document's `load` (a feed posted during the srcDoc swap may have gone to the previous document, or to this one before its resolver parsed — re-sending a key the resolver already holds is a no-op there, skipping one it lacks leaves that image black for the life of the document), advanced by every post. The XR popup, the A-Frame and Three.js tabs and the export still inline the `data:` URLs (a top-level or standalone document has no parent to feed it). `previewAssetFeed.test.ts` executes the resolver against the real Blob/URL/atob and source-pins all three surfaces; NOT yet run in a browser — see the S3 owner checks (Chrome and Safari, both backends, an image swap without a rebuild, a rebuild then a scrub). **The swap's no-op bail compares against the module the live document is RUNNING (`runningModuleRef` — seeded with `bakedModule` by the rebuild effect, advanced by every post), never against `bakedModule`**: a document is baked once and swapped many times, and the boot document is baked while the graph is still the empty red-sentinel shader, with the real graph arriving by swap — so NEW, whose module is that same sentinel, was skipped as "already there" and the preview kept the previous shader (2026-09-11), as did any undo back to the boot shader. The load-event replay DOES compare against `bakedModule`, correctly: a freshly loaded document is running exactly that. `previewRebuild.test.ts` pins both. The debounce still matters: `useDebounced(previewCode, 200)` (trailing, so first paint isn't delayed) collapses a scrub's pointermove stream into a single rebuild on release. A "Compiling shader…" overlay covers the gap, cleared by `fs:preview-ready` (success) **or** `fs:preview-error` (failure — a broken shader never reaches the ready handshake, since `checkShaderReady` polls for bound uniforms and just gives up), with a 12s failsafe so it can never stick over the error text.
- **Custom preview mesh**: dropping a `.obj`/`.glb`/`.gltf` on the 3D preview — or on the node canvas or the code panel, which both hand it to the preview's own `loadMeshFile` through `fs:preview-model-file` (utils/previewModelDrop.ts; a model dropped with a project follows the import, as on the preview) — (or importing a zip carrying one) loads it as the `custom` preview geometry. The mesh lives in the store's `previewMesh` slice — never history, the localStorage autosave, or the project embed (bytes can be tens of MB) — but IS mirrored to an **IndexedDB cache** (`utils/previewMeshCache.ts`) so it survives a reload; localStorage is the wrong store here (strings only, ~5-10 MB origin budget the graph autosave already fills). `setPreviewMesh` is the single persist chokepoint (fire-and-forget, resolves on every failure path so a quota error can't break a drop); the boot restore passes `{ persist: false }` since those bytes came out of the cache.
